Generated by `npm run review:f4-english` from `content/form-4/english/*.json`. Every question and drawing below was built by the engine.
All notes, teach cards, worked examples and paper tasks are **status: draft**. “Authored key” means a person fixed the
right answer (see `AUTHORED.md`); “computed by rule” means code worked it out (a formula, a conversion, a spelling rule).

Teach mode: cards (idea + example + 1 level-1 check) → worked example → practice (levels 1–3) → the note as a summary.

**Summary (the note)**

> A discussion of care should ask what support a person wants instead of assuming every older person, child or disabled person has identical needs. Use polite offers, relevant questions and clear reasons. An offer is not permission to ignore a refusal. Language practice uses fictional cases; pupils do not need to disclose private health or family information. It does not train a learner to administer treatment or take over an adult’s medical responsibilities.

## Lesson 2: Speaking- talk about strategies of taking care of different family members and friends

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e4-2 (Discussing care for family members and friends)

**Note** (draft)

> A discussion of care should ask what support a person wants instead of assuming every older person, child or disabled person has identical needs. Use polite offers, relevant questions and clear reasons. An offer is not permission to ignore a refusal. Language practice uses fictional cases; pupils do not need to disclose private health or family information. It does not train a learner to administer treatment or take over an adult’s medical responsibilities.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to offer and discuss respectful practical support without making assumptions about a person’s needs. Builds on: Form Three’s polite requests and reasoned suggestions. Leads to: family-life discussion and directed writing for an audience.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A discussion of care should ask what support a person wants instead of assuming every older person, child or disabled person has identical needs. Use polite offers, relevant questions and clear reasons. An offer is not permission to ignore a refusal. Language practice uses fictional cases; pupils do not need to disclose private health or family information. It does not train a learner to administer treatment or take over an adult’s medical responsibilities.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Bih asks an imaginary older neighbour, Would you like help with this light bag? The neighbour accepts and says where it should go. Ayuk asks a friend what would make a group activity easier to join. If a younger sibling seems unwell, the fictional learner tells a responsible adult instead of inventing treatment.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Prepare short turns: state the point, give a relevant reason and invite a response. Use fictional people if the subject is private. Practise with a partner when available; written choices here do not establish oral fluency or listening ability. Challenge an idea without insulting the speaker.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **polite offer**: a courteous proposal of assistance that leaves a choice
- **clarifying question**: a question establishing what support is actually needed
- **respectful language**: wording that preserves a person’s dignity
- **appropriate referral**: seeking a responsible adult or trained support for a concern beyond the learner’s role


Common mistake: “An offer means ignoring every refusal.” → “polite offer: a courteous proposal of assistance that leaves a choice.”. The person should be able to accept or decline.

## Lesson 3: Grammar- review of parts of speech and tenses (capsule table of tenses)

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e4-3 (Parts of speech and a tense overview)

**Note** (draft)

> A word’s class depends on its use: book is a noun in the book and a verb in book a room. Nouns name, verbs form the clause’s action or state, adjectives describe nouns and adverbs can modify actions or other expressions. Time and grammatical tense are related but not identical. Simple present often expresses habits; simple past presents completed past events. Progressive forms use be + -ing; perfect forms use have + past participle. This is an overview, not a claim that every form has only one meaning.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to identify word functions in context and distinguish common tense/aspect patterns. Builds on: Form Three’s sentence patterns and verb forms. Leads to: choosing accurate simple forms, clause patterns and narrative tenses.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A word’s class depends on its use: book is a noun in the book and a verb in book a room. Nouns name, verbs form the clause’s action or state, adjectives describe nouns and adverbs can modify actions or other expressions. Time and grammatical tense are related but not identical. Simple present often expresses habits; simple past presents completed past events. Progressive forms use be + -ing; perfect forms use have + past participle. This is an overview, not a claim that every form has only one meaning.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original capsule: Bih reads after chores: present habit. Yesterday she wrote a note: simple past. She is reading now: present progressive. She has written the note: present perfect. By the later meeting she had written it: past perfect. She will read tomorrow: future reference. Read the whole time context before choosing a form.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Ask what the word does in this sentence, then identify the finite verb and any auxiliary. Do not label every -ly word an adverb without checking its function. A past participle is not automatically the same as the simple past. Later lessons practise particular contrasts in more depth.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **noun**: a word naming an entity or idea in the stated use
- **verb**: a word functioning in the clause’s action or state pattern
- **adjective**: a word describing a noun in the stated pattern
- **adverb**: a word modifying how or when an action occurs in the stated pattern


Common mistake: “Book must be a verb in every sentence.” → “noun: a word naming an entity or idea in the stated use.”. Word class depends on use; the book uses book as a noun.

**Card e4-3-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _noun: a word naming an entity or idea in the stated use._  
> _verb: a word functioning in the clause’s action or state pattern._  
> _adjective: a word describing a noun in the stated pattern._  
> _adverb: a word modifying how or when an action occurs in the stated pattern._  

Check question: `e4-3-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a word naming an entity or idea in the stated use?

## Lesson 4: Grammar- the simple and past tense

Prerequisites: 3 · Skill: e4-4 (Simple present and simple past)

**Note** (draft)

> The simple present commonly expresses habits or general states; the simple past commonly presents a completed event in a past context. For a third-person singular present subject, ordinary verbs usually add -s or -es. Past negatives and questions with did use the base verb: did not go, not did not went. Be has its own forms. Time words guide interpretation but do not replace reading the meaning. Compare the present habit with the completed past event before choosing a form.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to choose between a present habit and a completed past event and form a past negative correctly. Builds on: parts of speech and the tense overview. Leads to: transitivity, future forms and later narrative tense contrasts.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> The simple present commonly expresses habits or general states; the simple past commonly presents a completed event in a past context. For a third-person singular present subject, ordinary verbs usually add -s or -es. Past negatives and questions with did use the base verb: did not go, not did not went. Be has its own forms. Time words guide interpretation but do not replace reading the meaning. Compare the present habit with the completed past event before choosing a form.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original contrast: Bih buys materials on market days. Yesterday she bought an item. She did not buy it the day before. The negative uses did to carry the past; bought must not be added after did. In a habit report, yesterday cannot simply be attached to the present form without changing the intended construction.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the time frame and whether the sentence is affirmative, negative or a question. Check subject agreement in the present and irregular forms in the past. Preserve the meaning when correcting; do not add two past markers around did.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **present habit**: a repeated activity described with the simple present
- **completed past event**: an action presented as finished in its past context
- **past negative**: did not followed by the base verb for an ordinary verb
- **present agreement**: the third-person singular form with an appropriate subject


Common mistake: “Every habitual sentence must be a past event.” → “present habit: a repeated activity described with the simple present.”. The simple present can report a repeated activity.

**Card e4-4-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _present habit: a repeated activity described with the simple present._  
> _completed past event: an action presented as finished in its past context._  
> _past negative: did not followed by the base verb for an ordinary verb._  
> _present agreement: the third-person singular form with an appropriate subject._

- ◻️ completed past event  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an action presented as finished in its past context, rather than the third-person singular form with an appropriate subject._

Full working after a second miss:

> The singular third-person subject requires the appropriate present form.  
> So the answer is “present agreement”.

## Lesson 5: Writing- introduction to directed writing

Prerequisites: 3, 4 · Skill: e4-5 (Introduction to directed writing)

**Note** (draft)

> Directed writing provides a situation and specific instructions. Identify your role, audience, purpose and required form before drafting. Underline every content point and decide where it belongs. A fluent response can still fail its purpose if it omits an essential instruction or uses the wrong format. Use a suitable tone and distinguish information given by the task from assumptions. Check the completed text against the directions rather than marking it by length alone.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to produce a response that fulfils the specified purpose, audience, form and content points. Builds on: sentence accuracy and familiar school communication. Leads to: essay planning, letters and later talks or reports.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Directed writing provides a situation and specific instructions. Identify your role, audience, purpose and required form before drafting. Underline every content point and decide where it belongs. A fluent response can still fail its purpose if it omits an essential instruction or uses the wrong format. Use a suitable tone and distinguish information given by the task from assumptions. Check the completed text against the directions rather than marking it by length alone.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: CLASSROOM MODEL NOTICE. Reading Club Meeting. Members are invited to a planning meeting in the school library on Monday afternoon. We will choose a shared reading activity and agree how to care for borrowed books. Please bring a suggestion and a notebook. Issued by the fictional club secretary. The model gives audience, place, time and purpose without claiming an actual school event.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Make a short checklist from the prompt. Plan the order, draft the required form and check each point. Add only suitable invented details where the task permits them. Do not replace a requested notice with an unrelated essay simply because its sentences are correct.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **purpose**: the result the writing is intended to achieve
- **audience**: the intended readers of the response
- **required form**: the kind of text specified in the instructions
- **content checklist**: a record of the points the prompt requires


Common mistake: “A response fulfils every task merely by containing correct spelling.” → “purpose: the result the writing is intended to achieve.”. Correct language must also serve the requested purpose.

**Card e4-5-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _purpose: the result the writing is intended to achieve._  
> _audience: the intended readers of the response._  
> _required form: the kind of text specified in the instructions._  
> _content checklist: a record of the points the prompt requires._  

Check question: `e4-5-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a record of the points the prompt requires?

- ◻️ audience  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the intended readers of the response, rather than a record of the points the prompt requires._
- ◻️ required form  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the kind of text specified in the instructions, rather than a record of the points the prompt requires._
- ✅ content checklist

## Lesson 6: Listening – listen to a passage on different studying skills

Prerequisites: 5 · Skill: e4-6 (Written preparation: studying-skills listening script)

**Note** (draft)

> This is written preparation for a listening lesson. Read the original script for meaning, key information and unknown words. It provides no audio and cannot assess listening comprehension. A teacher or recording should later supply an oral version; the questions here assess the written script. Identify the main idea and locate evidence for each answer. A question may concern a detail, reason, sequence or word in context. If the text omits information, do not supply an invented fact. A plausible outcome is not necessarily a reported result.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to extract the main idea and supporting details from an original study-skills script. Builds on: directed writing and organising task points. Leads to: later listening practice and evidence-based reading.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> This is written preparation for a listening lesson. Read the original script for meaning, key information and unknown words. It provides no audio and cannot assess listening comprehension. A teacher or recording should later supply an oral version; the questions here assess the written script. Identify the main idea and locate evidence for each answer. A question may concern a detail, reason, sequence or word in context. If the text omits information, do not supply an invented fact. A plausible outcome is not necessarily a reported result.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original script: Bih recognises definitions while reading but cannot explain them later. Ayuk suggests closing the book, recalling one idea and then checking it. They agree to alternate questions after household tasks. Bih says the first attempt may show gaps; correcting those gaps matters more than copying an answer. Their plan does not promise instant success or give a fixed study timetable for every pupil.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Point to a phrase supporting the answer and explain any inference. When summarising, preserve who did what and whether an action was planned or completed. These original classroom texts are not extracts from prescribed literature.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **explicit detail**: information directly stated in the passage
- **supported inference**: an interpretation linked to clues in the passage
- **main idea**: the central message organising the passage
- **information not supplied**: a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine


Common mistake: “An explicit detail is always something invented by the reader.” → “explicit detail: information directly stated in the passage.”. Direct information appears in the passage.

**Card e4-6-c1** (draft)

> _information not supplied: a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine._

## Lesson 7: Speech work: /ᶞ / and /θ/

Prerequisites: 3, 6 · Skill: e4-7 (Written preparation: /θ/ and /ð/)

**Note** (draft)

> Phonetic symbols represent sounds, not ordinary letter names. The pair contrasts a voiceless model with a voiced model. Voicing concerns vocal-fold vibration, not capitals or loudness. Both are dental fricatives: the tongue approaches the teeth and air passes with friction. The screen supplies written models only. Use a teacher or reliable recording to practise the actual sounds; printed choices cannot assess your pronunciation.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to group written model words by their initial consonant and explain the stated voicing distinction. Builds on: written sound-symbol preparation and careful dictionary use. Leads to: clearer word recognition and later teacher-led pronunciation practice.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Phonetic symbols represent sounds, not ordinary letter names. The pair contrasts a voiceless model with a voiced model. Voicing concerns vocal-fold vibration, not capitals or loudness. Both are dental fricatives: the tongue approaches the teeth and air passes with friction. The screen supplies written models only. Use a teacher or reliable recording to practise the actual sounds; printed choices cannot assess your pronunciation.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Written model groups: think, thin, thank, thing begin with /θ/; this, that, these, those begin with /ð/. Compare the initial consonant, not the word’s final letter. Different spellings can sometimes represent the same sound, so spelling alone is not a universal pronunciation rule. These are standard model examples to verify with the teacher’s pronunciation convention.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify which sound the model supplies, then group the written examples. For the actual contrast, listen to a model and practise separately with a teacher. Do not interpret a correct multiple-choice answer as proof that you have heard or produced the sound accurately. Word-initial examples avoid claiming that every occurrence of a letter combination behaves identically.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **voiceless model /θ/**: the initial consonant group represented by /θ/ in the examples
- **voiced model /ð/**: the initial consonant group represented by /ð/ in the examples
- **sound-symbol distinction**: the difference between phonetic notation and ordinary spelling
- **written preparation**: recognition of models before a separate audio or spoken activity


Common mistake: “Every word in this lesson begins with the voiced consonant.” → “voiceless model /θ/: the initial consonant group represented by /θ/ in the examples.”. The models separate the voiceless and voiced groups.

**Card e4-7-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _voiceless model /θ/: the initial consonant group represented by /θ/ in the examples._  
> _voiced model /ð/: the initial consonant group represented by /ð/ in the examples._  
> _sound-symbol distinction: the difference between phonetic notation and ordinary spelling._  
> _written preparation: recognition of models before a separate audio or spoken activity._  

Check question: `e4-7-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means the difference between phonetic notation and ordinary spelling?

- ◻️ voiced model /ð/  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the initial consonant group represented by /ð/ in the examples, rather than the difference between phonetic notation and ordinary spelling._
- ✅ sound-symbol distinction
- ◻️ voiceless model /θ/  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the initial consonant group represented by /θ/ in the examples, rather than the difference between phonetic notation and ordinary spelling._
- ◻️ written preparation  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns recognition of models before a separate audio or spoken activity, rather than the difference between phonetic notation and ordinary spelling._

Full working after a second miss:

> Check pronunciation rather than generalising the spelling without limits.  
> So the answer is “sound-symbol distinction”.  

**Card e4-7-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>

## Lesson 8: Writing- introduction to essay writing (drawing a plan, thesis statement, topic sentences)

Prerequisites: 5 · Skill: e4-8 (Essay planning, thesis statements and topic sentences)

**Note** (draft)

> An essay needs a focus and an order that helps the reader. A thesis statement presents the controlling idea or position; a topic sentence gives the main idea of a paragraph. Supporting details explain, illustrate or qualify that idea. A plan is an outline, not the final essay. An introduction should prepare the reader for the focus, while a conclusion draws together the developed ideas. Avoid claiming that every essay type needs an identical rigid formula.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to plan a focused essay with a controlling idea and paragraphs that develop it. Builds on: directed-writing purpose and sentence accuracy. Leads to: argumentative and narrative writing with an organised structure.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> An essay needs a focus and an order that helps the reader. A thesis statement presents the controlling idea or position; a topic sentence gives the main idea of a paragraph. Supporting details explain, illustrate or qualify that idea. A plan is an outline, not the final essay. An introduction should prepare the reader for the focus, while a conclusion draws together the developed ideas. Avoid claiming that every essay type needs an identical rigid formula.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: Original plan on reading clubs: Focus: a club can support reading when members participate. Paragraph idea: shared discussion clarifies understanding. Support: members compare reasons for an interpretation. Paragraph idea: organisation makes participation possible. Support: clear roles and suitable meeting times. Ending: the club’s value depends on active use. A sample topic sentence is Shared discussion helps members test their understanding.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> State one manageable focus. Group related points and give each paragraph a clear role. Check that evidence supports the paragraph idea and that the paragraph serves the overall focus. Do not confuse a paragraph’s opening sentence with the whole essay’s thesis.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **thesis statement**: the essay’s controlling idea or position
- **topic sentence**: the main idea guiding a paragraph
- **supporting detail**: an explanation or example developing a paragraph’s idea
- **plan**: an outline arranging ideas before drafting


Common mistake: “A thesis must list every possible unrelated topic.” → “thesis statement: the essay’s controlling idea or position.”. A controlling idea gives the essay focus.

**Card e4-8-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _thesis statement: the essay’s controlling idea or position._  
> _topic sentence: the main idea guiding a paragraph._  
> _supporting detail: an explanation or example developing a paragraph’s idea._  
> _plan: an outline arranging ideas before drafting._

- ◻️ supporting detail  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an explanation or example developing a paragraph’s idea, rather than the main idea guiding a paragraph._

Full working after a second miss:

> It guides the paragraph’s development.  
> So the answer is “topic sentence”.

## Lesson 9: Speaking- talk about aspects of family life education

Prerequisites: 2, 5 · Skill: e4-9 (Discussing aspects of family life)

**Note** (draft)

> Family-life discussions can examine cooperation, communication and differing responsibilities. Avoid claiming that every household has the same members or arrangements. Give reasons, listen to another view and distinguish a suggestion from a command. Learners can use fictional households and are not required to share private events. A disagreement should address the action or proposal rather than a person’s worth. Respectful language can still express a clear limit or seek help.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to exchange views about shared responsibilities, respect and communication using fictional situations. Builds on: polite offers, directed purpose and careful word choice. Leads to: reading an original family-life passage and writing to different audiences.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Family-life discussions can examine cooperation, communication and differing responsibilities. Avoid claiming that every household has the same members or arrangements. Give reasons, listen to another view and distinguish a suggestion from a command. Learners can use fictional households and are not required to share private events. A disagreement should address the action or proposal rather than a person’s worth. Respectful language can still express a clear limit or seek help.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original dialogue: In a fictional household, study time overlaps with chores. Bih proposes discussing the fixed deadlines. Ayuk says a fair plan must consider each person’s responsibilities and available time. They agree to ask a responsible adult to help adjust the arrangement. The exchange offers a possible process, not a judgment that every household must follow one schedule.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Prepare short turns: state the point, give a relevant reason and invite a response. Use fictional people if the subject is private. Practise with a partner when available; written choices here do not establish oral fluency or listening ability. Challenge an idea without insulting the speaker.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **shared responsibility**: work or duties considered together within the stated arrangement
- **reasoned suggestion**: a proposed action supported by an explanation
- **active listening**: attending to another person’s view before responding
- **respectful boundary**: a clear limit expressed without degrading another person


Common mistake: “Fairness always means identical tasks for every person regardless of circumstances.” → “shared responsibility: work or duties considered together within the stated arrangement.”. Responsibilities should be considered with the actual circumstances.

**Card e4-9-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _shared responsibility: work or duties considered together within the stated arrangement._  
> _reasoned suggestion: a proposed action supported by an explanation._  
> _active listening: attending to another person’s view before responding._  
> _respectful boundary: a clear limit expressed without degrading another person._  

Check question: `e4-9-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a proposed action supported by an explanation?

- ◻️ active listening  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns attending to another person’s view before responding, rather than a proposed action supported by an explanation._
- ✅ reasoned suggestion
- ◻️ shared responsibility  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns work or duties considered together within the stated arrangement, rather than a proposed action supported by an explanation._
- ◻️ respectful boundary  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a clear limit expressed without degrading another person, rather than a proposed action supported by an explanation._

Full working after a second miss:

> A useful proposal responds to the actual problem.  
> So the answer is “reasoned suggestion”.  

**Card e4-9-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _The household discusses how chores and study fit together._  
> _Discuss fixed deadlines because the activities overlap._  
> _Ayuk restates the concern before proposing an adjustment._  
> _A speaker declines to share private household details._  

Check question: `e4-9-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Discuss fixed deadlines because the activities overlap.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ respectful boundary

## Lesson 10: Reading: read a text on family life education

Prerequisites: 2, 9 · Skill: e4-10 (Reading an original family-life passage)

**Note** (draft)

> Reading involves following what a passage states, connecting its ideas and distinguishing supported inference from invention. Identify the main idea and locate evidence for each answer. A question may concern a detail, reason, sequence or word in context. If the text omits information, do not supply an invented fact. A plausible outcome is not necessarily a reported result.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to identify a problem, proposed response and limits of the evidence in a family-life passage. Builds on: respectful discussion and shared-responsibility language. Leads to: letters, argument and clear reports of information.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Reading involves following what a passage states, connecting its ideas and distinguishing supported inference from invention. Identify the main idea and locate evidence for each answer. A question may concern a detail, reason, sequence or word in context. If the text omits information, do not supply an invented fact. A plausible outcome is not necessarily a reported result.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original passage: In an imaginary household in Bamenda, Bih’s revision time overlaps with a younger sibling’s reading practice. She asks a responsible adult to help discuss the schedule. The family lists fixed tasks and available time before proposing an adjustment. The passage does not say that every household has the same arrangement or that the new plan has already solved every difficulty.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Point to a phrase supporting the answer and explain any inference. When summarising, preserve who did what and whether an action was planned or completed. These original classroom texts are not extracts from prescribed literature.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **explicit detail**: information directly stated in the passage
- **supported inference**: an interpretation linked to clues in the passage
- **main idea**: the central message organising the passage
- **information not supplied**: a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine


Common mistake: “An explicit detail is always something invented by the reader.” → “explicit detail: information directly stated in the passage.”. Direct information appears in the passage.

**Card e4-10-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _explicit detail: information directly stated in the passage._  
> _supported inference: an interpretation linked to clues in the passage._  
> _main idea: the central message organising the passage._  
> _information not supplied: a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine._  

Check question: `e4-10-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ✅ main idea
- ◻️ information not supplied  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine, rather than the central message organising the passage._
- ◻️ explicit detail  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns information directly stated in the passage, rather than the central message organising the passage._

Full working after a second miss:

> The main idea must represent the relevant whole.  
> So the answer is “main idea”.

## Lesson 11: Grammar- Transitive and intransitive verbs

Prerequisites: 3, 4 · Skill: e4-11 (Transitive and intransitive verb uses)

**Note** (draft)

> A transitive use takes a direct object: Bih opened the box. An intransitive use has no direct object: the box opened. A verb can allow both patterns, so classify the use in the sentence rather than attaching one permanent label to every spelling. A place or time phrase is not automatically a direct object. Linking complements also need a different analysis: happy in she seems happy describes the subject rather than naming an object acted on.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to identify a direct object and distinguish transitive from intransitive uses in context. Builds on: word functions and finite clause patterns. Leads to: adverbials, clause structure and accurate sentence construction.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A transitive use takes a direct object: Bih opened the box. An intransitive use has no direct object: the box opened. A verb can allow both patterns, so classify the use in the sentence rather than attaching one permanent label to every spelling. A place or time phrase is not automatically a direct object. Linking complements also need a different analysis: happy in she seems happy describes the subject rather than naming an object acted on.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original pairs: Ayuk writes a report; Ayuk writes after supper. The report is a direct object in the first use. After supper gives time in the second. Bih opened the door; the door opened. The change affects the pattern even though the verb is the same. Do not insert an object into arrive merely because another verb accepts one.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Find the verb use, then ask whether a noun phrase functions as its direct object. Distinguish object from an adverbial phrase or subject complement. Test the complete clause and its meaning instead of counting how many words follow the verb.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **transitive use**: a verb use taking a direct object
- **intransitive use**: a verb use without a direct object
- **adverbial phrase**: a phrase giving circumstances such as time or place
- **subject complement**: an expression describing or identifying the subject after a linking verb


Common mistake: “Any word after a verb must be its direct object.” → “transitive use: a verb use taking a direct object.”. A direct object has a grammatical role, not merely a position after a verb.

**Card e4-11-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _transitive use: a verb use taking a direct object._  
> _intransitive use: a verb use without a direct object._  
> _adverbial phrase: a phrase giving circumstances such as time or place._  
> _subject complement: an expression describing or identifying the subject after a linking verb._  

Check question: `e4-11-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a phrase giving circumstances such as time or place?

- ◻️ intransitive use  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a verb use without a direct object, rather than a phrase giving circumstances such as time or place._
- ◻️ subject complement  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an expression describing or identifying the subject after a linking verb, rather than a phrase giving circumstances such as time or place._
- ✅ adverbial phrase
- ◻️ transitive use  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a verb use taking a direct object, rather than a phrase giving circumstances such as time or place._

Full working after a second miss:

> The phrase states when, rather than what is written.  
> So the answer is “adverbial phrase”.  

**Card e4-11-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _Bih opened the box; consider the verb’s relation to the box._  
> _The box opened; no direct object follows opened._  
> _Ayuk writes after supper; after supper gives time._  
> _Bih seems happy; happy describes Bih._  

Check question: `e4-11-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> The box opened; no direct object follows opened.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ✅ intransitive use
- ◻️ adverbial phrase  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a phrase giving circumstances such as time or place. The selected feature instead illustrates a verb use without a direct object._
- ◻️ transitive use  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a verb use taking a direct object. The selected feature instead illustrates a verb use without a direct object._
- ◻️ subject complement  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means an expression describing or identifying the subject after a linking verb. The selected feature instead illustrates a verb use without a direct object._

Full working after a second miss:

> An intransitive use can be complete without a direct object.  
> So the answer is “intransitive use”.  

**Card e4-11-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _Any word after a verb must be its direct object. Correction: A direct object has a grammatical role, not merely a position after a verb._  
> _A verb with no direct object can never form a complete clause. Correction: An intransitive use can be complete without a direct object._  
> _After supper is necessarily the direct object of writes. Correction: The phrase states when, rather than what is written._  
> _Happy is always a direct object receiving an action in seems happy. Correction: The linking pattern relates the description to the subject._  

Check question: `e4-11-repair-card` · spot the error, level 1 · **authored key**

> Find the incorrect claim or language form.

- ✅ Happy is always a direct object receiving an action in seems happy.  
  _↳ explanation: The linking pattern relates the description to the subject._
- ◻️ adverbial phrase: a phrase giving circumstances such as time or place.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: a phrase giving circumstances such as time or place. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ transitive use: a verb use taking a direct object.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: a verb use taking a direct object. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ subject complement: an expression describing or identifying the subject after a linking verb.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: an expression describing or identifying the subject after a linking verb. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “Happy is always a direct object receiving an action in seems happy.”.  
> The linking pattern relates the description to the subject.

## Lesson 12: Grammar- Adverb of frequency, place and manner

Prerequisites: 11 · Skill: e4-12 (Adverbs of frequency, place and manner)

**Note** (draft)

> Frequency tells how often, place tells where and manner tells how an action happens. Often, outside and carefully illustrate these functions. A common frequency position is before an ordinary main verb but after be: Bih often reads; she is often early. Manner and place positions depend on the clause and emphasis. Some standard alternatives are possible, so a preferred model is not the only grammatical order. Not every -ly word is an adverb, and not every adverb ends in -ly.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to identify what an adverb contributes and choose a suitable position in a clear model sentence. Builds on: word classes, verb uses and adverbial phrases. Leads to: precise description and narrative detail.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Frequency tells how often, place tells where and manner tells how an action happens. Often, outside and carefully illustrate these functions. A common frequency position is before an ordinary main verb but after be: Bih often reads; she is often early. Manner and place positions depend on the clause and emphasis. Some standard alternatives are possible, so a preferred model is not the only grammatical order. Not every -ly word is an adverb, and not every adverb ends in -ly.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: Ayuk often studies outside. He reads carefully. Often describes frequency, outside location and carefully manner. Compare a careful reader with reads carefully: the adjective describes the noun, while the adverb modifies the action. Never already has negative force in the neutral standard pattern used here.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Ask which question the expression answers: how often, where or how. Check its relation to the verb and the rest of the sentence. Avoid unnecessary double negatives and preserve meaning when moving an adverb. Accept a standard alternative order when context supports it.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **frequency**: how often an event happens
- **place**: where an event occurs
- **manner**: how an action is performed
- **adjective/adverb contrast**: choosing a describing form according to what it modifies


Common mistake: “Often tells the colour of the book.” → “frequency: how often an event happens.”. Often describes recurrence, not colour.

**Card e4-12-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _frequency: how often an event happens._  
> _place: where an event occurs._  
> _manner: how an action is performed._  
> _adjective/adverb contrast: choosing a describing form according to what it modifies._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns where an event occurs, rather than how often an event happens._
- ◻️ adjective/adverb contrast  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns choosing a describing form according to what it modifies, rather than how often an event happens._

Full working after a second miss:

> Often describes recurrence, not colour.  
> So the answer is “frequency”.

## Lesson 13: Writing: Formal and informal letter

Prerequisites: 5, 8, 12 · Skill: e4-13 (Formal and informal letters)

**Note** (draft)

> A letter’s relationship and purpose affect its tone. A formal school request uses respectful clear wording, a suitable salutation, organised content and an appropriate close. An informal letter to a friend can sound more personal while remaining clear. Include the addresses, date and other elements the task and examination convention require; exact layouts may vary. Do not treat contractions as universally forbidden or friendliness as an excuse for missing the purpose. Use fictional addresses in classroom practice.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to write a letter whose layout, tone and content fit its reader and purpose. Builds on: directed-writing instructions and paragraph planning. Leads to: talks, reports and controlled changes of register.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A letter’s relationship and purpose affect its tone. A formal school request uses respectful clear wording, a suitable salutation, organised content and an appropriate close. An informal letter to a friend can sound more personal while remaining clear. Include the addresses, date and other elements the task and examination convention require; exact layouts may vary. Do not treat contractions as universally forbidden or friendliness as an excuse for missing the purpose. Use fictional addresses in classroom practice.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: Formal miniature: Dear Sir or Madam, I am writing on behalf of our fictional reading club to request permission to use the library after lessons. We propose supervised discussion and careful return of materials. Please let us know the arrangements required. Yours faithfully, Bih. Informal miniature: Dear Ayuk, Our club is planning a reading afternoon. I hope you can join us and bring a story suggestion. Write and tell me what you think. Best wishes, Bih.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Plan the request or message before adding the conventional frame. Match salutation and close, give the reason and state the response wanted. For the paper task, add fictitious address/date details in the teacher’s preferred layout. Compare tone without inventing one universal layout for every context.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **formal register**: language suited to an official or less familiar relationship
- **informal register**: language suited to a familiar personal relationship
- **salutation and close**: the greeting and ending chosen for the letter’s relationship and convention
- **requested response**: what the letter asks the reader to do or answer


Common mistake: “Formal means using insults and unnecessarily difficult words.” → “formal register: language suited to an official or less familiar relationship.”. Clarity and respect support a formal request.

**Card e4-13-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _formal register: language suited to an official or less familiar relationship._  
> _informal register: language suited to a familiar personal relationship._  
> _salutation and close: the greeting and ending chosen for the letter’s relationship and convention._  
> _requested response: what the letter asks the reader to do or answer._  

Check question: `e4-13-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means what the letter asks the reader to do or answer?

- ◻️ formal register  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns language suited to an official or less familiar relationship, rather than what the letter asks the reader to do or answer._
- ✅ requested response
- ◻️ salutation and close  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the greeting and ending chosen for the letter’s relationship and convention, rather than what the letter asks the reader to do or answer._
- ◻️ informal register  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns language suited to a familiar personal relationship, rather than what the letter asks the reader to do or answer._

Full working after a second miss:

> The reader needs a clear requested action.  
> So the answer is “requested response”.

>

> _Bih writes a friendly invitation to Ayuk._  
> _The model pairs Dear Sir or Madam with Yours faithfully._

> _A request letter should never indicate what answer or action it seeks. Correction: The reader needs a clear requested action._

**Answer:** formal register

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Using wholly fictional address/date details, write a formal letter requesting use of a school room, then a short informal letter inviting a friend to the planned activity. Give reasons and the response wanted.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Formal miniature: Dear Sir or Madam, I am writing on behalf of our fictional reading club to request permission to use the library after lessons. We propose supervised discussion and careful return of materials. Please let us know the arrangements required. Yours faithfully, Bih. Informal miniature: Dear Ayuk, Our club is planning a reading afternoon. I hope you can join us and bring a story suggestion. Write and tell me what you think. Best wishes, Bih.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] Each letter fits its audience and purpose.

## Lesson 14: Speaking: talk about different club and association activities

Prerequisites: 9, 12, 13 · Skill: e4-14 (Discussing club and association activities)

**Note** (draft)

> A useful club discussion combines an opinion with a reason and an example. Preferences do not establish universal facts. Ask a precise question when an activity is unclear, and acknowledge another view before responding. A proposal suggests what could happen; it does not report an event already completed. This is written preparation for speaking. A partner or teacher can later assess actual turn-taking, voice and listening, which the screen does not hear.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to express a club preference with reasons, ask for clarification and propose a shared activity. Builds on: family-life discussion, adverbs and letter purpose. Leads to: future plans and argumentative writing about school participation.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A useful club discussion combines an opinion with a reason and an example. Preferences do not establish universal facts. Ask a precise question when an activity is unclear, and acknowledge another view before responding. A proposal suggests what could happen; it does not report an event already completed. This is written preparation for speaking. A partner or teacher can later assess actual turn-taking, voice and listening, which the screen does not hear.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Bih prefers the reading club because members compare stories. Ayuk prefers the environmental club because he enjoys organising practical projects. Bih asks whether the groups could share an exhibition. Ayuk suggests checking space and supervision first. Their preferences remain different, while the proposal creates a possible shared task.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Prepare short turns: state the point, give a relevant reason and invite a response. Use fictional people if the subject is private. Practise with a partner when available; written choices here do not establish oral fluency or listening ability. Challenge an idea without insulting the speaker.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **opinion**: a stated personal view or preference
- **reason**: an explanation supporting the stated view
- **clarification**: a question seeking a more exact explanation
- **proposal**: an action suggested for consideration


Common mistake: “One preference proves every learner wants the same activity.” → “opinion: a stated personal view or preference.”. A preference describes the speaker’s view, not a universal fact.

**Card e4-14-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _opinion: a stated personal view or preference._  
> _reason: an explanation supporting the stated view._  
> _clarification: a question seeking a more exact explanation._  
> _proposal: an action suggested for consideration._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a question seeking a more exact explanation, rather than an explanation supporting the stated view._
- ◻️ proposal  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an action suggested for consideration, rather than an explanation supporting the stated view._
- ◻️ opinion  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a stated personal view or preference, rather than an explanation supporting the stated view._

Full working after a second miss:

> The reason must bear on the preference.  
> So the answer is “reason”.

## Lesson 15: Grammar- simple future tense

Prerequisites: 4 · Skill: e4-15 (Future reference with will and other forms)

**Note** (draft)

> Will + base verb can express a prediction, offer or decision, depending on context. Do not add -s or to to the lexical verb after will. Future reference also uses other patterns: be going to for a prior intention or evidence-based prediction, present progressive for an arrangement and simple present for a timetable. These uses overlap in real language. An exercise should specify its intended context rather than mark every alternative future form wrong.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to form will plus a base verb and choose a future expression suited to its context. Builds on: the simple present/past contrast and auxiliary patterns. Leads to: conditional sentences about possible future situations.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Will + base verb can express a prediction, offer or decision, depending on context. Do not add -s or to to the lexical verb after will. Future reference also uses other patterns: be going to for a prior intention or evidence-based prediction, present progressive for an arrangement and simple present for a timetable. These uses overlap in real language. An exercise should specify its intended context rather than mark every alternative future form wrong.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Bih notices the books on the floor and says, I will help. Ayuk already has a plan: I am going to organise the shelf. Their agreed meeting is tomorrow, while the printed timetable gives a fixed opening time. Each choice presents the future differently; will does not automatically mean a guaranteed fact.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify prediction, immediate offer, prior plan, arrangement or timetable. Check the auxiliary and base form. A question with will places it before the subject. Keep the same meaning when correcting the form rather than inventing a new intention.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **will + base verb**: a future-reference pattern without an added -s or to
- **immediate offer**: a willingness to act expressed in response to the situation
- **prior intention**: a plan already formed before the current exchange
- **timetable reference**: future reference using a scheduled event


Common mistake: “Bih will helps is the standard pattern.” → “will + base verb: a future-reference pattern without an added -s or to.”. The lexical verb stays in its base form after will.

**Card e4-15-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _will + base verb: a future-reference pattern without an added -s or to._  
> _immediate offer: a willingness to act expressed in response to the situation._  
> _prior intention: a plan already formed before the current exchange._  
> _timetable reference: future reference using a scheduled event._  

Check question: `e4-15-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a willingness to act expressed in response to the situation?

- ◻️ timetable reference  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns future reference using a scheduled event, rather than a willingness to act expressed in response to the situation._
- ◻️ will + base verb  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a future-reference pattern without an added -s or to, rather than a willingness to act expressed in response to the situation._
- ✅ immediate offer
- ◻️ prior intention  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a plan already formed before the current exchange, rather than a willingness to act expressed in response to the situation._

Full working after a second miss:

> The speaker offers a response at that point.  
> So the answer is “immediate offer”.  

**Card e4-15-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _Bih will help with the books._

> _Ayuk says he is going to organise the shelf he planned to sort._

- ◻️ immediate offer  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a willingness to act expressed in response to the situation. The selected feature instead illustrates a future-reference pattern without an added -s or to._

Full working after a second miss:

> The lexical verb stays in its base form after will.  
> So the answer is “will + base verb”.  

**Card e4-15-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _Bih will helps is the standard pattern. Correction: The lexical verb stays in its base form after will._  
> _An immediate offer must always describe a completed past event. Correction: The speaker offers a response at that point._  
> _Every future plan can only be expressed with one form in every context. Correction: Going to is a useful model for the prior intention, without excluding all alternatives._  
> _A present form can never refer to a scheduled future event. Correction: Simple present can describe timetable events._

## Lesson 16: Vocabulary: context clue and collocation

Prerequisites: 3, 12 · Skill: e4-16 (Context clues and collocation)

**Note** (draft)

> A context clue is evidence from surrounding language. A definition, contrast, example or consequence can help explain an unfamiliar word. The clue supports a meaning; it does not justify any guess that sounds interesting. A collocation is a conventional word combination, such as make a decision or heavy rain. A synonym may fit a definition yet sound unnatural with a particular partner. Check grammar and meaning together, and use a dictionary when the context is insufficient.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to infer a supported word meaning from context and choose a natural word combination. Builds on: word classes and sentence meaning. Leads to: careful reading of trade passages and precise writing.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A context clue is evidence from surrounding language. A definition, contrast, example or consequence can help explain an unfamiliar word. The clue supports a meaning; it does not justify any guess that sounds interesting. A collocation is a conventional word combination, such as make a decision or heavy rain. A synonym may fit a definition yet sound unnatural with a particular partner. Check grammar and meaning together, and use a dictionary when the context is insufficient.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original example: The path was slippery, so Bih moved carefully to avoid falling. The consequence supports a surface on which slipping is easy. A club can make a decision and do homework, but exchanging make and do mechanically creates unnatural combinations. Heavy rain is ordinary wording; strong rain is not the neutral model taught here.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Locate the exact clue and explain how it supports your interpretation. Separate a contextual inference from a fact not given. When choosing a collocation, consider the whole phrase rather than the isolated meaning of one word.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **definition clue**: surrounding wording that explains the unfamiliar expression
- **contrast clue**: opposed wording that helps reveal a meaning
- **collocation**: a conventional combination of words
- **supported inference**: a conclusion linked to clues actually provided


Common mistake: “A definition clue requires no explanatory wording.” → “definition clue: surrounding wording that explains the unfamiliar expression.”. The nearby explanation supplies the meaning.

**Card e4-16-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _definition clue: surrounding wording that explains the unfamiliar expression._  
> _contrast clue: opposed wording that helps reveal a meaning._  
> _collocation: a conventional combination of words._  
> _supported inference: a conclusion linked to clues actually provided._  

Check question: `e4-16-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns opposed wording that helps reveal a meaning, rather than surrounding wording that explains the unfamiliar expression._
- ◻️ collocation  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a conventional combination of words, rather than surrounding wording that explains the unfamiliar expression._
- ◻️ supported inference  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a conclusion linked to clues actually provided, rather than surrounding wording that explains the unfamiliar expression._

Full working after a second miss:

> The nearby explanation supplies the meaning.  
> So the answer is “definition clue”.

> _Any synonym can replace any word in a collocation naturally. Correction: Conventional combinations constrain the choice._  
> _A guess with no textual clue is automatically proven. Correction: An inference must connect to evidence in the passage._

## Lesson 17: Grammar: Conditional sentences

Prerequisites: 4, 15 · Skill: e4-17 (Conditional sentences)

**Note** (draft)

> A conditional links a condition and a result. Common zero-condition models use present forms for general patterns. A first-condition model uses if + present and will + base for a possible future. A second-condition model commonly uses if + past and would + base for an imagined present or future. A third-condition model uses if + past perfect and would have + participle for an unreal past. These are useful patterns, not a claim that every possible conditional must fit one rigid template.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to match a common conditional pattern to its meaning and correct a malformed clause. Builds on: past, present and future-reference forms. Leads to: reasoned argument and later imagined or past situations.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A conditional links a condition and a result. Common zero-condition models use present forms for general patterns. A first-condition model uses if + present and will + base for a possible future. A second-condition model commonly uses if + past and would + base for an imagined present or future. A third-condition model uses if + past perfect and would have + participle for an unreal past. These are useful patterns, not a claim that every possible conditional must fit one rigid template.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original models: If the room is available, the club meets there as a general rule. If it is available tomorrow, we will meet there. If we had another room, we would share the work. If we had booked earlier, we would have obtained the room. The last situation is imagined about the past; it does not report that the earlier booking actually happened.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Choose the intended time and degree of reality before the form. In the ordinary first-condition model, do not insert will into the if-clause simply because the time is future. Other modal uses exist, so a special context can justify another construction. Clause order can change without reversing the condition and result.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **zero-condition model**: a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship
- **first-condition model**: a common if-present/will-base pattern for a possible future
- **second-condition model**: an if-past/would-base pattern for an imagined present or future
- **third-condition model**: an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past


Common mistake: “Every conditional necessarily concerns one future event.” → “zero-condition model: a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship.”. A zero-condition model can describe a general pattern.

**Card e4-17-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _zero-condition model: a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship._  
> _first-condition model: a common if-present/will-base pattern for a possible future._  
> _second-condition model: an if-past/would-base pattern for an imagined present or future._  
> _third-condition model: an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past._  

Check question: `e4-17-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship?

- ◻️ third-condition model  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past, rather than a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship._
- ✅ zero-condition model
- ◻️ first-condition model  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a common if-present/will-base pattern for a possible future, rather than a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship._
- ◻️ second-condition model  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an if-past/would-base pattern for an imagined present or future, rather than a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship._

Full working after a second miss:

> A zero-condition model can describe a general pattern.  
> So the answer is “zero-condition model”.  

**Card e4-17-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _If the room is available, the club meets there under its general rule._  
> _If the room is free tomorrow, we will meet there._  
> _If we had another room now, we would use it._  
> _If we had booked earlier, we would have obtained the room._  

Check question: `e4-17-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ◻️ zero-condition model  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship. The selected feature instead illustrates an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means an if-past/would-base pattern for an imagined present or future. The selected feature instead illustrates an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past._
- ✅ third-condition model

Full working after a second miss:

> The model imagines a past alternative, rather than asserting its occurrence.  
> So the answer is “third-condition model”.  

**Card e4-17-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _Every conditional necessarily concerns one future event. Correction: A zero-condition model can describe a general pattern._  
> _The ordinary first-condition model always requires will in both clauses. Correction: Use present in the if-clause and will plus base in the result here._  
> _The past form here proves the imagined room existed yesterday. Correction: The past form can mark hypothetical distance rather than past time._  
> _The third-condition model reports that the earlier condition actually happened. Correction: The model imagines a past alternative, rather than asserting its occurrence._

> Choose the imagined present/future model.

- ◻️ If Ayuk has more time, he would to read.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Would does not take to in this pattern._
- ◻️ If Ayuk had more time, he would reads.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Use base read after would._
- ◻️ If Ayuk had more time, he will reading.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Will reading does not form the requested model._
- ✅ If Ayuk had more time, he would read.

Full working after a second miss:

> If-past plus would-base fits the stated imagined situation.  
> So the answer is “If Ayuk had more time, he would read.”.  

> Choose the ordinary possible-future model.

- ✅ If Bih arrives early, she will help.
- ◻️ If Bih will arrives early, she will help.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The ordinary if-clause uses present, with correct agreement._
- ◻️ If Bih arrive early, she will helped.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Arrives and help are required in this pattern._
- ◻️ If Bih arrived yesterday, she will helped yesterday.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: This mixes incompatible time and verb patterns._

Full working after a second miss:

> If-present links to will plus base.  
> So the answer is “If Bih arrives early, she will help.”.  

**Practice** `e4-17-link` · matching, level 2 · **authored key**

> Match each term to its example.

Right-hand side (shuffled): If the room is free tomorrow, we will meet there. · If we had another room now, we would use it. · If we had booked earlier, we would have obtained the room.
- third-condition model → **If we had booked earlier, we would have obtained the room.**
- first-condition model → **If the room is free tomorrow, we will meet there.**
- second-condition model → **If we had another room now, we would use it.**

Full working after a second miss:

> The right pairs are:  
> third-condition model → If we had booked earlier, we would have obtained the room.  
> first-condition model → If the room is free tomorrow, we will meet there.  
> second-condition model → If we had another room now, we would use it.  

> Match each term to its example.

Right-hand side (shuffled): If we had booked earlier, we would have obtained the room. · If the room is free tomorrow, we will meet there. · If the room is available, the club meets there under its general rule.
- first-condition model → **If the room is free tomorrow, we will meet there.**
- zero-condition model → **If the room is available, the club meets there under its general rule.**
- third-condition model → **If we had booked earlier, we would have obtained the room.**

Full working after a second miss:

> The right pairs are:  
> first-condition model → If the room is free tomorrow, we will meet there.  
> zero-condition model → If the room is available, the club meets there under its general rule.  
> third-condition model → If we had booked earlier, we would have obtained the room.

- ◻️ third-condition model: an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past.

- ◻️ second-condition model: an if-past/would-base pattern for an imagined present or future.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: an if-past/would-base pattern for an imagined present or future. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ✅ The past form here proves the imagined room existed yesterday.  
  _↳ explanation: The past form can mark hypothetical distance rather than past time._

## Lesson 18: Grammar: Preposition of time, place, purpose and direction

Prerequisites: 12, 17 · Skill: e4-18 (Prepositions of time, place, purpose and direction)

**Note** (draft)

> Prepositions express relations such as time, location or movement. In a simple school model, at can mark a clock time, on a day/date and in a longer period. In and on can locate something inside a space or on a surface; to can mark destination and into movement inside. For + noun can express purpose or intended use; to + base verb is an infinitive purpose construction, not the same grammatical pattern. Fixed combinations and context create exceptions, so do not apply a single word rule everywhere.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to choose a preposition or related purpose construction from the meaning of the whole phrase. Builds on: adverbials and clause relationships. Leads to: accurate descriptions, reports and movement narratives.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Prepositions express relations such as time, location or movement. In a simple school model, at can mark a clock time, on a day/date and in a longer period. In and on can locate something inside a space or on a surface; to can mark destination and into movement inside. For + noun can express purpose or intended use; to + base verb is an infinitive purpose construction, not the same grammatical pattern. Fixed combinations and context create exceptions, so do not apply a single word rule everywhere.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: The club meets on Monday in the afternoon. Bih waits at the entrance, walks into the room and puts a notebook on the table. A shelf is for books; members gather to read. The direction into differs from a position already in the room. Read the complete relation before choosing a small word.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Ask whether the phrase gives time, position, movement or purpose. Check what follows: for a noun, to a base verb in the purpose model. Do not confuse the prepositional use of to before a destination with every infinitive use of to.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **time relation**: a phrase locating an event in a temporal frame
- **position**: a location without asserting movement into it
- **direction**: a relation showing movement towards or into a place
- **purpose construction**: wording expressing intended use or aim


Common mistake: “On Monday gives the direction of movement.” → “time relation: a phrase locating an event in a temporal frame.”. The named day locates time rather than destination.

**Card e4-18-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _time relation: a phrase locating an event in a temporal frame._  
> _position: a location without asserting movement into it._  
> _direction: a relation showing movement towards or into a place._  
> _purpose construction: wording expressing intended use or aim._  

Check question: `e4-18-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a relation showing movement towards or into a place?

- ◻️ position  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a location without asserting movement into it, rather than a relation showing movement towards or into a place._
- ◻️ time relation  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a phrase locating an event in a temporal frame, rather than a relation showing movement towards or into a place._
- ✅ direction
- ◻️ purpose construction  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns wording expressing intended use or aim, rather than a relation showing movement towards or into a place._

Full working after a second miss:

> Into expresses the movement inside here.  
> So the answer is “direction”.  

**Card e4-18-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _The club meets on Monday._  
> _The notebook is on the table._

## Lesson 19: Speech work: the sound /ts/ and /ǳ/

Prerequisites: 7, 16 · Skill: e4-19 (Written preparation: /tʃ/ and /dʒ/)

**Note** (draft)

> Phonetic symbols represent sounds, not ordinary letter names. The pair contrasts a voiceless model with a voiced model. Voicing concerns vocal-fold vibration, not capitals or loudness. Both are affricates: a brief closure releases into friction. They are not the plain /s/ and /z/ fricatives, made near the ridge behind the upper teeth. The screen supplies written models only. Use a teacher or reliable recording to practise the actual sounds; printed choices cannot assess your pronunciation.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to group written model words by their initial consonant and explain the stated voicing distinction. Builds on: written sound-symbol preparation and careful dictionary use. Leads to: clearer word recognition and later teacher-led pronunciation practice.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Phonetic symbols represent sounds, not ordinary letter names. The pair contrasts a voiceless model with a voiced model. Voicing concerns vocal-fold vibration, not capitals or loudness. Both are affricates: a brief closure releases into friction. They are not the plain /s/ and /z/ fricatives, made near the ridge behind the upper teeth. The screen supplies written models only. Use a teacher or reliable recording to practise the actual sounds; printed choices cannot assess your pronunciation.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Written model groups: chair, cheap, chalk, choose begin with /tʃ/; judge, jam, joy, join begin with /dʒ/. Compare the initial consonant, not the word’s final letter. Different spellings can sometimes represent the same sound, so spelling alone is not a universal pronunciation rule. These are standard model examples to verify with the teacher’s pronunciation convention.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify which sound the model supplies, then group the written examples. For the actual contrast, listen to a model and practise separately with a teacher. Do not interpret a correct multiple-choice answer as proof that you have heard or produced the sound accurately. Word-initial examples avoid claiming that every occurrence of a letter combination behaves identically.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **voiceless model /tʃ/**: the initial consonant group represented by /tʃ/ in the examples
- **voiced model /dʒ/**: the initial consonant group represented by /dʒ/ in the examples
- **sound-symbol distinction**: the difference between phonetic notation and ordinary spelling
- **written preparation**: recognition of models before a separate audio or spoken activity


Common mistake: “Every word in this lesson begins with the voiced consonant.” → “voiceless model /tʃ/: the initial consonant group represented by /tʃ/ in the examples.”. The models separate the voiceless and voiced groups.

**Card e4-19-c1** (draft)

> _voiced model /dʒ/: the initial consonant group represented by /dʒ/ in the examples._  
> _sound-symbol distinction: the difference between phonetic notation and ordinary spelling._  
> _written preparation: recognition of models before a separate audio or spoken activity._

- ◻️ voiceless model /tʃ/

Full working after a second miss:

> Voicing concerns vocal-fold vibration, not letter case.  
> So the answer is “voiced model /dʒ/”.
