## Lesson 20: Reading: read a text on E-commerce versus new trade, Vocabulary related to E- commerce

Prerequisites: 16, 18 · Skill: e4-20 (Reading: e-commerce and face-to-face trade)

**Note** (draft)

> Reading involves following what a passage states, connecting its ideas and distinguishing supported inference from invention. Identify the main idea and locate evidence for each answer. A question may concern a detail, reason, sequence or word in context. If the text omits information, do not supply an invented fact. A plausible outcome is not necessarily a reported result.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to compare the passage’s trading arrangements and interpret its vocabulary without assuming unreported prices or outcomes. Builds on: context clues, collocations and reasoned comparison. Leads to: argumentative writing and checking claims about transactions.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Reading involves following what a passage states, connecting its ideas and distinguishing supported inference from invention. Identify the main idea and locate evidence for each answer. A question may concern a detail, reason, sequence or word in context. If the text omits information, do not supply an invented fact. A plausible outcome is not necessarily a reported result.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original passage: An imaginary Douala seller displays goods online and at a stall. An online customer asks about the item, total price and delivery before deciding. A stall customer can inspect the displayed item but still needs clear information. The seller keeps a transaction record. Online ordering can extend contact beyond the stall, yet a listing alone proves neither product quality nor guaranteed delivery. No actual price or platform is named.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Point to a phrase supporting the answer and explain any inference. When summarising, preserve who did what and whether an action was planned or completed. These original classroom texts are not extracts from prescribed literature.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **explicit detail**: information directly stated in the passage
- **supported inference**: an interpretation linked to clues in the passage
- **main idea**: the central message organising the passage
- **information not supplied**: a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine


Common mistake: “An explicit detail is always something invented by the reader.” → “explicit detail: information directly stated in the passage.”. Direct information appears in the passage.

**Card e4-20-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _explicit detail: information directly stated in the passage._  
> _supported inference: an interpretation linked to clues in the passage._  
> _main idea: the central message organising the passage._  
> _information not supplied: a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine._

- ◻️ explicit detail

- ◻️ supported inference  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an interpretation linked to clues in the passage, rather than a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine._

Full working after a second miss:

> A faithful answer acknowledges missing information.  
> So the answer is “information not supplied”.

> _The reader explains a possible intention using described actions._  
> _A summary brings together the important actions without unrelated additions._  
> _The reader refuses to invent a price or result that is absent._

## Lesson 21: Grammar: sentence types (declarative, imperative, Interrogative and exclamative)

Prerequisites: 3, 11 · Skill: e4-21 (Sentence types and communicative purpose)

**Note** (draft)

> A declarative normally states information; an interrogative normally asks a question; an imperative can give an instruction or request; an exclamative expresses a strong reaction through its construction. Form and communicative purpose can differ: Could you close the door? is interrogative in form but can function as a polite request. An exclamation mark alone does not turn every sentence into an exclamative structure. Read the grammar and situation, not merely the final sign.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to distinguish declarative, interrogative, imperative and exclamative forms without relying on punctuation alone. Builds on: verb and clause patterns in statements and questions. Leads to: argumentative writing and sentence-structure analysis.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A declarative normally states information; an interrogative normally asks a question; an imperative can give an instruction or request; an exclamative expresses a strong reaction through its construction. Form and communicative purpose can differ: Could you close the door? is interrogative in form but can function as a polite request. An exclamation mark alone does not turn every sentence into an exclamative structure. Read the grammar and situation, not merely the final sign.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original models: Bih has the book. Has Bih brought it? Bring the book, please. What a useful book it is! The imperative normally omits the subject you. A question can also request an action, so identify whether an exercise asks for grammatical form or communicative purpose.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Find the finite verb, subject pattern and any imperative or exclamative construction. Label form and function separately when needed. Use punctuation to support the construction instead of letting it decide everything by itself.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **declarative**: a statement-form clause in the model
- **interrogative**: a question-form clause in the model
- **imperative**: a directive form normally without an expressed you subject
- **exclamative**: a construction expressing a strong reaction, such as what a or how


Common mistake: “Every declarative must ask a direct question.” → “declarative: a statement-form clause in the model.”. This form states information.

**Card e4-21-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _declarative: a statement-form clause in the model._  
> _interrogative: a question-form clause in the model._  
> _imperative: a directive form normally without an expressed you subject._  
> _exclamative: a construction expressing a strong reaction, such as what a or how._  

Check question: `e4-21-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a question-form clause in the model?

- ◻️ imperative  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a directive form normally without an expressed you subject, rather than a question-form clause in the model._
- ✅ interrogative
- ◻️ exclamative  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a construction expressing a strong reaction, such as what a or how, rather than a question-form clause in the model._
- ◻️ declarative  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a statement-form clause in the model, rather than a question-form clause in the model._

Full working after a second miss:

> A question form can function as a request in context.  
> So the answer is “interrogative”.  

**Card e4-21-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _Bih has the book._  
> _Has Bih brought the book?_  
> _Bring the book, please._  
> _What a useful book it is!_  

Check question: `e4-21-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Bih has the book.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ interrogative  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a question-form clause in the model. The selected feature instead illustrates a statement-form clause in the model._
- ◻️ imperative  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a directive form normally without an expressed you subject. The selected feature instead illustrates a statement-form clause in the model._
- ✅ declarative
- ◻️ exclamative  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a construction expressing a strong reaction, such as what a or how. The selected feature instead illustrates a statement-form clause in the model._

Full working after a second miss:

> This form states information.  
> So the answer is “declarative”.  

**Card e4-21-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _Every declarative must ask a direct question. Correction: This form states information._  
> _A polite request can never use interrogative form. Correction: A question form can function as a request in context._  
> _An imperative always needs you written before the base verb. Correction: The directive model normally leaves you unexpressed._  
> _Any sentence becomes an exclamative structure solely by adding an exclamation mark. Correction: The construction, not just the mark, matters._

- ✅ Every declarative must ask a direct question.

- ◻️ interrogative: a question-form clause in the model.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: a question-form clause in the model. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

Full working after a second miss:

> This form states information.

## Lesson 22: Writing: Argumentative essay

Prerequisites: 8, 16, 17, 21 · Skill: e4-22 (Argumentative essays)

**Note** (draft)

> An argumentative essay defends a position through reasons. A claim needs support that bears on it; repeating a preference is not evidence. A counterargument presents a relevant objection, and a response explains how the writer addresses it. A conclusion should follow the discussion rather than introduce an unsupported new case. An argument can be firm without insulting opponents. Do not invent statistics, expert quotations or universal outcomes merely to make a paragraph sound persuasive.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to develop a position with reasons, relevant support and a fair response to a counterargument. Builds on: essay focus, contextual evidence and conditional reasoning. Leads to: stronger paragraph structure and balanced formal argument.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> An argumentative essay defends a position through reasons. A claim needs support that bears on it; repeating a preference is not evidence. A counterargument presents a relevant objection, and a response explains how the writer addresses it. A conclusion should follow the discussion rather than introduce an unsupported new case. An argument can be firm without insulting opponents. Do not invent statistics, expert quotations or universal outcomes merely to make a paragraph sound persuasive.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: Original model: A school reading club can be useful when it provides structured discussion. Members can compare interpretations and explain their reasons rather than merely exchange answers. Some may object that meetings take time from individual study. This concern matters, so a club should use suitable meeting times and focused tasks. Participation should be purposeful, not compulsory busywork. A well-organised club can therefore complement individual reading, although its value depends on how members use it.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Choose a clear position and outline reasons with examples. Present an objection accurately before answering it. Qualify a claim when the evidence has limits. Check whether the conclusion is supported and whether the reader can distinguish an invented illustration from a verified statistic.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **position**: the main view the writer defends
- **relevant support**: a reason or example bearing on the stated claim
- **counterargument**: a relevant objection to the writer’s view
- **reasoned response**: an answer explaining how an objection is addressed or limits the view


Common mistake: “An argument must change to an unrelated position in every paragraph.” → “position: the main view the writer defends.”. A clear position organises the reasoning.

**Card e4-22-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _position: the main view the writer defends._  
> _relevant support: a reason or example bearing on the stated claim._  
> _counterargument: a relevant objection to the writer’s view._  
> _reasoned response: an answer explaining how an objection is addressed or limits the view._  

Check question: `e4-22-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ◻️ relevant support  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a reason or example bearing on the stated claim, rather than a relevant objection to the writer’s view._
- ◻️ position  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the main view the writer defends, rather than a relevant objection to the writer’s view._
- ◻️ reasoned response  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an answer explaining how an objection is addressed or limits the view, rather than a relevant objection to the writer’s view._
- ✅ counterargument

Full working after a second miss:

> An objection can address costs or reasoning without attacking a person.  
> So the answer is “counterargument”.

> So the answer is “relevant support”.

## Lesson 23: Grammar- sentence structure (simple, compound, complex and compound complex)

Prerequisites: 17, 21 · Skill: e4-23 (Simple, compound, complex and compound-complex sentences)

**Note** (draft)

> A simple sentence has one independent clause; it can still have a compound subject or verb. A compound sentence has at least two independent clauses. A complex sentence has an independent clause and at least one dependent clause. A compound-complex sentence combines at least two independent clauses with a dependent clause. Length and the mere presence of and do not determine the type. A comma alone does not normally join independent clauses in formal writing; use an appropriate conjunction or punctuation.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to classify sentence structure by independent and dependent clauses and repair a comma splice. Builds on: sentence types, conjunctions and finite clause patterns. Leads to: organised narratives and varied formal writing.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A simple sentence has one independent clause; it can still have a compound subject or verb. A compound sentence has at least two independent clauses. A complex sentence has an independent clause and at least one dependent clause. A compound-complex sentence combines at least two independent clauses with a dependent clause. Length and the mere presence of and do not determine the type. A comma alone does not normally join independent clauses in formal writing; use an appropriate conjunction or punctuation.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original set: Bih and Ayuk read quietly: simple. Bih reads, and Ayuk writes: compound. Bih reads because the room is quiet: complex. Bih reads because the room is quiet, and Ayuk writes: compound-complex. Count clauses by their structure, not names or verbs in a list.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Mark the boundaries, identify clauses that can stand independently and note dependent markers. Check whether and joins words, phrases or clauses. When repairing a comma splice, preserve the intended relationship rather than inserting any connector at random.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **simple sentence**: a sentence with one independent clause
- **compound sentence**: a sentence with at least two independent clauses and no dependent clause in this classification
- **complex sentence**: a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause
- **compound-complex sentence**: a sentence combining multiple independent clauses with a dependent clause


Common mistake: “Every sentence containing and is compound.” → “simple sentence: a sentence with one independent clause.”. And can join subjects within a single clause.

**Card e4-23-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _simple sentence: a sentence with one independent clause._  
> _compound sentence: a sentence with at least two independent clauses and no dependent clause in this classification._  
> _complex sentence: a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause._  
> _compound-complex sentence: a sentence combining multiple independent clauses with a dependent clause._  

Check question: `e4-23-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause?

- ◻️ compound-complex sentence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a sentence combining multiple independent clauses with a dependent clause, rather than a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause._
- ✅ complex sentence
- ◻️ simple sentence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a sentence with one independent clause, rather than a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause._
- ◻️ compound sentence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a sentence with at least two independent clauses and no dependent clause in this classification, rather than a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause._

Full working after a second miss:

> The dependent clause needs its main clause in this model.  
> So the answer is “complex sentence”.  

**Card e4-23-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _Bih and Ayuk read quietly._  
> _Bih reads, and Ayuk writes._  
> _Bih reads because the room is quiet._  
> _Bih reads because it is quiet, and Ayuk writes._  

Check question: `e4-23-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Bih reads because the room is quiet.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ simple sentence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a sentence with one independent clause. The selected feature instead illustrates a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause._
- ◻️ compound-complex sentence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a sentence combining multiple independent clauses with a dependent clause. The selected feature instead illustrates a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause._
- ◻️ compound sentence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a sentence with at least two independent clauses and no dependent clause in this classification. The selected feature instead illustrates a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause._
- ✅ complex sentence

Full working after a second miss:

> The dependent clause needs its main clause in this model.  
> So the answer is “complex sentence”.  

**Card e4-23-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>

> _A because-clause alone is always a complete independent sentence. Correction: The dependent clause needs its main clause in this model._  
> _Sentence length alone proves that a sentence is compound-complex. Correction: The number and dependence of clauses decide the classification._

  _↳ explanation: This is correct: a sentence with one independent clause. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

  _↳ explanation: This is correct: a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

## Lesson 24: Vocabulary- homonyms, homophones and homographs

Prerequisites: 16 · Skill: e4-24 (Homonyms, homophones and homographs)

**Note** (draft)

> Homophones share pronunciation while differing in meaning; their spellings may differ, as with hear and here. Homographs share spelling but differ in meaning and may differ in pronunciation, as with lead a group and lead the metal. Homonym has both a broad dictionary use and a narrower school use for words sharing spelling and pronunciation with different meanings. This lesson states the narrower use when needed. Categories can overlap, so do not force one exclusive label where the question has not specified its criterion.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to distinguish sound, spelling and meaning while recognising that terminology can overlap. Builds on: context clues, word classes and written sound symbols. Leads to: editing narrative vocabulary and avoiding wrong-word spellings.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Homophones share pronunciation while differing in meaning; their spellings may differ, as with hear and here. Homographs share spelling but differ in meaning and may differ in pronunciation, as with lead a group and lead the metal. Homonym has both a broad dictionary use and a narrower school use for words sharing spelling and pronunciation with different meanings. This lesson states the narrower use when needed. Categories can overlap, so do not force one exclusive label where the question has not specified its criterion.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original set: Hear the speaker here. A bat can be an animal or sports equipment in the narrow homonym model. Lead a group differs from lead the metal in the pronunciation model. Spell-check alone may not detect a word that is spelled correctly but has the wrong meaning for the sentence.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Ask whether the exercise compares sound, spelling or both. Read the context to identify meaning. The screen can compare written examples and transcriptions, but actual pronunciation needs a teacher or recording. Confirm the school’s preferred homonym terminology.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **homophone relation**: shared pronunciation with different meanings
- **homograph relation**: shared spelling with different meanings
- **narrow homonym use**: shared spelling and pronunciation with different meanings in the specified school model
- **contextual word choice**: selecting the meaning and spelling required by the sentence


Common mistake: “Homophones must have identical meanings.” → “homophone relation: shared pronunciation with different meanings.”. The shared sound does not make their meanings identical.

**Card e4-24-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _homophone relation: shared pronunciation with different meanings._  
> _homograph relation: shared spelling with different meanings._  
> _narrow homonym use: shared spelling and pronunciation with different meanings in the specified school model._  
> _contextual word choice: selecting the meaning and spelling required by the sentence._

## Lesson 25: Writing- Narrative essay

Prerequisites: 8, 23 · Skill: e4-25 (Narrative essays)

**Note** (draft)

> A narrative tells linked events from a chosen viewpoint. Establish the situation, introduce a change or difficulty, develop actions and provide an ending that relates to them. Time signals, pronoun reference and verb choices help the reader follow the sequence. Description should support the events, not interrupt them with an unrelated catalogue. A narrative may be invented; pupils need not disclose private incidents. A dramatic story still needs consistency in what characters know and do.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to write a coherent original narrative with setting, linked events and a meaningful ending. Builds on: paragraph planning, sentence structure and simple-past events. Leads to: past-perfect chronology and later report/narrative contrasts.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A narrative tells linked events from a chosen viewpoint. Establish the situation, introduce a change or difficulty, develop actions and provide an ending that relates to them. Time signals, pronoun reference and verb choices help the reader follow the sequence. Description should support the events, not interrupt them with an unrelated catalogue. A narrative may be invented; pupils need not disclose private incidents. A dramatic story still needs consistency in what characters know and do.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: Original model: Bih reached the fictional club room and found the notice missing. Members were gathering outside, unsure whether the meeting would happen. She checked with the teacher instead of announcing a cancellation. The room was ready; the notice had fallen behind a shelf. Bih replaced it and helped members enter. The small misunderstanding showed her why checking a source mattered more than spreading a quick guess.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Plan the setting, difficulty, actions and outcome. Choose a viewpoint and preserve it. Make transitions clear and reread each pronoun. Invent suitable details if you prefer, but do not change an object’s location or a character’s knowledge accidentally just to force the ending.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **setting**: the place, time and circumstances establishing a narrative
- **complication**: a change or difficulty setting actions in motion
- **causal sequence**: events linked by understandable reasons and consequences
- **resolution**: the outcome addressing the narrative’s difficulty


Common mistake: “A narrative must give no clues about where events occur.” → “setting: the place, time and circumstances establishing a narrative.”. Relevant context helps a reader follow the events.

**Card e4-25-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _setting: the place, time and circumstances establishing a narrative._  
> _complication: a change or difficulty setting actions in motion._  
> _causal sequence: events linked by understandable reasons and consequences._  
> _resolution: the outcome addressing the narrative’s difficulty._  

Check question: `e4-25-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means the outcome addressing the narrative’s difficulty?

- ◻️ causal sequence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns events linked by understandable reasons and consequences, rather than the outcome addressing the narrative’s difficulty._
- ✅ resolution
- ◻️ setting  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the place, time and circumstances establishing a narrative, rather than the outcome addressing the narrative’s difficulty._
- ◻️ complication  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a change or difficulty setting actions in motion, rather than the outcome addressing the narrative’s difficulty._

Full working after a second miss:

> The ending relates to the difficulty developed.  
> So the answer is “resolution”.

>

## Lesson 26: Speaking- talk and write numbers in figures and words, list different countries and their currencies

Prerequisites: 24, 25 · Skill: e4-26 (Numbers, figures, words and currency names)

**Note** (draft)

> Figures represent numbers with numerals; words express the same number in language. Read place value and zeros carefully, then preserve the currency unit. An amount in CFA francs is not automatically the same purchasing value as the same numeral in another currency. This lesson names currencies but supplies no exchange-rate exercise or payment instruction. Number-word conventions can vary; the model follows the written model shown here and the teacher should confirm accepted examination styles.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to match a number phrase to its figure and name the currencies in a checked country list. Builds on: word meaning, written forms and place-value knowledge. Leads to: a written money script and precise quantities in reports.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Figures represent numbers with numerals; words express the same number in language. Read place value and zeros carefully, then preserve the currency unit. An amount in CFA francs is not automatically the same purchasing value as the same numeral in another currency. This lesson names currencies but supplies no exchange-rate exercise or payment instruction. Number-word conventions can vary; the model follows the written model shown here and the teacher should confirm accepted examination styles.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model amount: 12 500 CFA francs is twelve thousand five hundred CFA francs. Country examples: Cameroon uses the Central African CFA franc, XAF; Nigeria the naira, NGN; Ghana the Ghana cedi, GHS; the United Kingdom pound sterling, GBP. Codes help distinguish currencies with similar names. These names do not imply that any two currencies can be interchanged without considering conversion.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Group digits by place value, read the full phrase and check the unit. When speaking is practised later, a teacher should check how the amount is pronounced; this screen checks written recognition. Do not infer a current exchange rate from a country/currency list.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **figures**: a numerical written representation
- **number words**: a linguistic written representation of a number
- **place value**: the value determined by a digit’s position
- **currency label**: the unit identifying which money an amount refers to


Common mistake: “Figures means a person’s bank signature.” → “figures: a numerical written representation.”. Figures represent a number rather than identifying a signatory.

**Card e4-26-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _figures: a numerical written representation._  
> _number words: a linguistic written representation of a number._  
> _place value: the value determined by a digit’s position._  
> _currency label: the unit identifying which money an amount refers to._  

Check question: `e4-26-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means the value determined by a digit’s position?

- ◻️ currency label  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the unit identifying which money an amount refers to, rather than the value determined by a digit’s position._
- ✅ place value
- ◻️ number words  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a linguistic written representation of a number, rather than the value determined by a digit’s position._
- ◻️ figures  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a numerical written representation, rather than the value determined by a digit’s position._

Full working after a second miss:

> A zero can change the place values of the other digits.  
> So the answer is “place value”.  

**Card e4-26-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _The model writes an amount using numerals._  
> _The phrase twelve thousand five hundred represents the model amount._  
> _The learner checks zeros before accepting an amount._  
> _The amount names CFA francs instead of leaving the unit unknown._

- ◻️ currency label

- ◻️ place value  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means the value determined by a digit’s position. The selected feature instead illustrates a linguistic written representation of a number._
- ◻️ figures  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a numerical written representation. The selected feature instead illustrates a linguistic written representation of a number._
- ✅ number words

Full working after a second miss:

> Both representations should express the same value.  
> So the answer is “number words”.  

**Card e4-26-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _Figures means a person’s bank signature. Correction: Figures represent a number rather than identifying a signatory._  
> _Words and figures may disagree without changing the amount. Correction: Both representations should express the same value._  
> _An added zero never changes a number’s value. Correction: A zero can change the place values of the other digits._  
> _Identical numerals in every currency necessarily buy the same amount. Correction: Currency identity and value cannot be inferred from the numeral alone._

## Lesson 27: Listening- listen to a passage on numbers and amount of money

Prerequisites: 26 · Skill: e4-27 (Written preparation: a money-and-amount listening script)

**Note** (draft)

> This is written preparation for a listening lesson. Read the original script for meaning, key information and unknown words. It provides no audio and cannot assess listening comprehension. A teacher or recording should later supply an oral version; the questions here assess the written script. Identify the main idea and locate evidence for each answer. A question may concern a detail, reason, sequence or word in context. If the text omits information, do not supply an invented fact. A plausible outcome is not necessarily a reported result.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to extract an amount and transaction sequence from a written script before later listening practice. Builds on: figures, number words and currency labels. Leads to: clear numerical references in talks and reports.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> This is written preparation for a listening lesson. Read the original script for meaning, key information and unknown words. It provides no audio and cannot assess listening comprehension. A teacher or recording should later supply an oral version; the questions here assess the written script. Identify the main idea and locate evidence for each answer. A question may concern a detail, reason, sequence or word in context. If the text omits information, do not supply an invented fact. A plausible outcome is not necessarily a reported result.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original script: In a fictional school exercise, Bih brings 5000 CFA francs. A notebook costs 1500 CFA francs and a folder costs 1000. She chooses the notebook and postpones the folder. The model therefore leaves 3500 CFA francs. All amounts are invented. The script gives no real seller or bank details and supplies no exchange rate.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Point to a phrase supporting the answer and explain any inference. When summarising, preserve who did what and whether an action was planned or completed. These original classroom texts are not extracts from prescribed literature.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **explicit detail**: information directly stated in the passage
- **supported inference**: an interpretation linked to clues in the passage
- **main idea**: the central message organising the passage
- **information not supplied**: a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine


Common mistake: “An explicit detail is always something invented by the reader.” → “explicit detail: information directly stated in the passage.”. Direct information appears in the passage.

**Card e4-27-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _explicit detail: information directly stated in the passage._  
> _supported inference: an interpretation linked to clues in the passage._  
> _main idea: the central message organising the passage._  
> _information not supplied: a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine._  

Check question: `e4-27-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means information directly stated in the passage?

- ✅ explicit detail
- ◻️ main idea  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the central message organising the passage, rather than information directly stated in the passage._
- ◻️ supported inference  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an interpretation linked to clues in the passage, rather than information directly stated in the passage._
- ◻️ information not supplied  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine, rather than information directly stated in the passage._

Full working after a second miss:

> Direct information appears in the passage.  
> So the answer is “explicit detail”.  

**Card e4-27-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _The reader repeats a place or action the passage actually states._  
> _The reader explains a possible intention using described actions._  
> _A summary brings together the important actions without unrelated additions._  
> _The reader refuses to invent a price or result that is absent._  

Check question: `e4-27-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> A summary brings together the important actions without unrelated additions.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ information not supplied  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine. The selected feature instead illustrates the central message organising the passage._
- ✅ main idea
- ◻️ supported inference  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means an interpretation linked to clues in the passage. The selected feature instead illustrates the central message organising the passage._
- ◻️ explicit detail  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means information directly stated in the passage. The selected feature instead illustrates the central message organising the passage._

Full working after a second miss:

> The main idea must represent the relevant whole.  
> So the answer is “main idea”.  

**Card e4-27-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _An explicit detail is always something invented by the reader. Correction: Direct information appears in the passage._  
> _Any private guess is a proven inference. Correction: An inference needs textual support._  
> _A random minor word always gives the complete main idea. Correction: The main idea must represent the relevant whole._  
> _An absent detail must always be replaced with a guessed number. Correction: A faithful answer acknowledges missing information._

> Original script: In a fictional school exercise, Bih brings 5000 CFA francs. A notebook costs 1500 CFA francs and a folder costs 1000. She chooses the notebook and postpones the folder. The model therefore leaves 3500 CFA francs. All amounts are invented. The script gives no real seller or bank details and supplies no exchange rate.

  _↳ feedback if chosen: Spending reduces, rather than increases, the balance._

> Original script: In a fictional school exercise, Bih brings 5000 CFA francs. A notebook costs 1500 CFA francs and a folder costs 1000. She chooses the notebook and postpones the folder. The model therefore leaves 3500 CFA francs. All amounts are invented. The script gives no real seller or bank details and supplies no exchange rate.
> Which detail is absent?

- ◻️ The notebook’s stated price.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The model price is given._
- ◻️ The starting amount.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The starting amount is given._
- ◻️ The fact that the folder is postponed.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Postponement is explicit._
- ✅ A real seller’s identity.

Full working after a second miss:

> The script uses fictional amounts and no real seller.  
> So the answer is “A real seller’s identity.”.  

**Practice** `e4-27-evidence` · multiple choice, level 2 · **authored key**

> Check this reading claim about the original passage: A missing price or name can be guessed and reported as stated fact.

- ◻️ Yes: every guess is an explicit detail.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A guess is not direct information._
- ◻️ Yes: a price from another story must apply.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A different story supplies no fact about this passage._
- ✅ No: report only what the passage supplies or supports.
- ◻️ Yes: a familiar name must be the writer’s chosen name.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Familiarity does not provide textual evidence._

Full working after a second miss:

> Missing information stays unestablished.  
> So the answer is “No: report only what the passage supplies or supports.”.  

> Check this reading claim about the original passage: A proposed action proves that the action has already been completed.

- ✅ No: a proposal and a completed event are different.

- ◻️ Yes: every suggestion is a reported result.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A suggestion is not a result._

## Lesson 28: Writing- A talk

Prerequisites: 5, 13, 22 · Skill: e4-28 (Writing a talk for an audience)

**Note** (draft)

> A written talk is meant to be heard by a particular audience. A greeting can establish the relationship; a clear opening gives the topic and purpose. Develop a few connected points using language listeners can follow. Signposts such as first and finally can help without replacing actual reasoning. A conclusion draws the message together and may propose a suitable action. This task prepares the script; a text-only screen cannot assess pace, voice, eye contact or listening response.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to prepare a talk with an appropriate address, clear message, supporting points and a purposeful ending. Builds on: directed writing, letters and argumentative paragraph organisation. Leads to: spoken delivery practice and later reports for different audiences.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A written talk is meant to be heard by a particular audience. A greeting can establish the relationship; a clear opening gives the topic and purpose. Develop a few connected points using language listeners can follow. Signposts such as first and finally can help without replacing actual reasoning. A conclusion draws the message together and may propose a suitable action. This task prepares the script; a text-only screen cannot assess pace, voice, eye contact or listening response.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: Original model: Fellow learners, our reading room belongs to everyone who uses it. Today I ask us to keep its materials useful. First, return each book to the agreed place so another reader can find it. Next, report damaged materials rather than hiding the problem. Finally, listen when others explain the borrowing arrangements. Small organised actions can make shared reading easier. Let us agree on responsibilities and review how well they work. Thank you.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> State audience, occasion and purpose before drafting. Use examples and direct language suited to listeners. Read the script with a partner or teacher if possible, but do not confuse a completed written task with an assessed oral performance. Preserve factual accuracy even when seeking a memorable phrase.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **audience address**: the opening acknowledgement suited to the listeners
- **signposting**: wording that guides listeners through the ideas
- **supporting example**: a concrete case making the message understandable
- **purposeful ending**: a close that draws together the message or suitable action


Common mistake: “Every talk must begin with a private bank account number.” → “audience address: the opening acknowledgement suited to the listeners.”. The opening should establish a suitable audience relationship.

> _signposting: wording that guides listeners through the ideas._  
> _supporting example: a concrete case making the message understandable._  
> _purposeful ending: a close that draws together the message or suitable action._

## Lesson 29: Grammar- Past tense and past perfect tense

Prerequisites: 4, 25 · Skill: e4-29 (Simple past and past perfect)

**Note** (draft)

> The simple past can tell a completed past sequence. The past perfect uses had + past participle to look back from a later past point. It helps show an earlier event or situation relevant at that point. Do not use had plus the simple-past form when the participle differs. Past perfect is not required for every earlier event if order is already clear; use it to make the intended perspective clear rather than marking ordinary chronological narration wrong.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to show the order of past events with simple past and had plus a participle. Builds on: simple past and the overview of perfect forms. Leads to: clear chronology in narratives and reports.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> The simple past can tell a completed past sequence. The past perfect uses had + past participle to look back from a later past point. It helps show an earlier event or situation relevant at that point. Do not use had plus the simple-past form when the participle differs. Past perfect is not required for every earlier event if order is already clear; use it to make the intended perspective clear rather than marking ordinary chronological narration wrong.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: When Ayuk arrived, Bih had written the note. Writing came before the later arrival. Compare Bih wrote the note and then Ayuk arrived, which directly narrates the sequence. Had written supplies the look-back relationship; had wrote does not give the standard form.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Choose the later past reference point, identify the earlier event and select a participle. Keep the same event order when rewriting. A sequence word such as before can already clarify order, so context should determine whether past perfect is needed or simply useful.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **simple-past sequence**: completed events narrated in their stated order
- **past perfect**: had plus a past participle looking back from a past point
- **later reference point**: the past event from which an earlier situation is viewed
- **chronological signal**: wording clarifying the order of events


Common mistake: “A past narrative cannot use more than one simple-past verb.” → “simple-past sequence: completed events narrated in their stated order.”. Simple past can narrate a chronological sequence.

**Card e4-29-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _simple-past sequence: completed events narrated in their stated order._  
> _past perfect: had plus a past participle looking back from a past point._  
> _later reference point: the past event from which an earlier situation is viewed._  
> _chronological signal: wording clarifying the order of events._  

Check question: `e4-29-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means had plus a past participle looking back from a past point?

- ◻️ later reference point  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the past event from which an earlier situation is viewed, rather than had plus a past participle looking back from a past point._
- ✅ past perfect
- ◻️ simple-past sequence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns completed events narrated in their stated order, rather than had plus a past participle looking back from a past point._
- ◻️ chronological signal  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns wording clarifying the order of events, rather than had plus a past participle looking back from a past point._

Full working after a second miss:

> Had takes the participle written.  
> So the answer is “past perfect”.  

**Card e4-29-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _Bih wrote the note and then Ayuk arrived._  
> _Bih had written the note before the later arrival._  
> _Ayuk’s arrival is the later point in when he arrived, Bih had written._  
> _Then and before can help show the sequence._

- ◻️ simple-past sequence

- ◻️ later reference point  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means the past event from which an earlier situation is viewed. The selected feature instead illustrates wording clarifying the order of events._

Full working after a second miss:

> Context and intended perspective determine the tense choice.  
> So the answer is “chronological signal”.  

**Card e4-29-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _A past narrative cannot use more than one simple-past verb. Correction: Simple past can narrate a chronological sequence._  
> _Had wrote is the standard perfect form of write. Correction: Had takes the participle written._  
> _The later reference point must always occur in the future. Correction: Both events can be past; the reference point is later within that past._  
> _Every earlier action must use past perfect even when the order is already clear. Correction: Context and intended perspective determine the tense choice._

**Practice** `e4-29-repair` · spot the error, level 3 · **authored key**

> Find the incorrect claim or language form.

- ✅ Had wrote is the standard perfect form of write.  
  _↳ explanation: Had takes the participle written._
- ◻️ past perfect: had plus a past participle looking back from a past point.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: had plus a past participle looking back from a past point. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ chronological signal: wording clarifying the order of events.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: wording clarifying the order of events. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ later reference point: the past event from which an earlier situation is viewed.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: the past event from which an earlier situation is viewed. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “Had wrote is the standard perfect form of write.”.  
> Had takes the participle written.  

> Find the incorrect claim or language form.

- ◻️ past perfect: had plus a past participle looking back from a past point.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: had plus a past participle looking back from a past point. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ✅ The later reference point must always occur in the future.  
  _↳ explanation: Both events can be past; the reference point is later within that past._
- ◻️ simple-past sequence: completed events narrated in their stated order.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: completed events narrated in their stated order. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ later reference point: the past event from which an earlier situation is viewed.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: the past event from which an earlier situation is viewed. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “The later reference point must always occur in the future.”.  
> Both events can be past; the reference point is later within that past.  

## Lesson 30: Speaking- talk about ways of protecting the environment

Prerequisites: 14, 15, 28 · Skill: e4-30 (Discussing environmental protection)

**Note** (draft)

> An environmental discussion should connect a specific action to its purpose. Reducing waste prevents some waste at source; reusing gives an appropriate object another use; recycling requires a suitable treatment route. Sorting alone does not prove a material is recycled. Use the school’s actual instructions for practical work and keep unknown or dangerous waste outside pupils’ handling tasks. This lesson practises language through fictional proposals rather than arranging an unsupervised activity.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to propose a realistic environmental action, explain its purpose and recognise a limitation. Builds on: reasoned discussion, future expressions and talks for an audience. Leads to: word-building, reports and evidence-based hygiene/safety reading.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> An environmental discussion should connect a specific action to its purpose. Reducing waste prevents some waste at source; reusing gives an appropriate object another use; recycling requires a suitable treatment route. Sorting alone does not prove a material is recycled. Use the school’s actual instructions for practical work and keep unknown or dangerous waste outside pupils’ handling tasks. This lesson practises language through fictional proposals rather than arranging an unsupervised activity.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Bih proposes avoiding unnecessary printing. Ayuk suggests reusing a clean box for materials. Another member asks which materials the local collection arrangement accepts. The group will check the teacher’s instructions before acting. Each proposal explains a possible benefit without claiming that one small action solves every environmental problem.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Prepare short turns: state the point, give a relevant reason and invite a response. Use fictional people if the subject is private. Practise with a partner when available; written choices here do not establish oral fluency or listening ability. Challenge an idea without insulting the speaker.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **reduction**: avoiding part of the resource use or waste at source
- **reuse**: giving an appropriate object another use
- **recycling**: transforming a material through a suitable treatment route
- **qualified proposal**: a suggested action recognising relevant conditions or limits


Common mistake: “Reduction necessarily means producing more waste.” → “reduction: avoiding part of the resource use or waste at source.”. The aim is to prevent part of the use or waste.

**Card e4-30-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _reduction: avoiding part of the resource use or waste at source._  
> _reuse: giving an appropriate object another use._  
> _recycling: transforming a material through a suitable treatment route._  
> _qualified proposal: a suggested action recognising relevant conditions or limits._

- ◻️ qualified proposal  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a suggested action recognising relevant conditions or limits, rather than transforming a material through a suitable treatment route._
- ◻️ reduction  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns avoiding part of the resource use or waste at source, rather than transforming a material through a suitable treatment route._

Full working after a second miss:

> Collection, sorting and actual processing are different stages.  
> So the answer is “recycling”.

## Lesson 31: Vocabulary- prefixes and suffixes, synonyms and antonyms

Prerequisites: 16, 24 · Skill: e4-31 (Prefixes, suffixes, synonyms and antonyms)

**Note** (draft)

> A prefix attaches before a base, while a suffix attaches after it. Either can affect meaning, and suffixes can change word class. Unhappy uses a negative prefix; helpful uses a suffix to form an adjective. Not every visible letter sequence is a productive affix. Synonyms have related meanings in context, while antonyms oppose meanings on a relevant scale. A word may have several senses, so neither a prefix nor a dictionary synonym permits every possible substitution.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to use a word’s structure and context to choose a related form or meaning. Builds on: word classes, contextual clues and homonym distinctions. Leads to: editing reports and building precise vocabulary.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A prefix attaches before a base, while a suffix attaches after it. Either can affect meaning, and suffixes can change word class. Unhappy uses a negative prefix; helpful uses a suffix to form an adjective. Not every visible letter sequence is a productive affix. Synonyms have related meanings in context, while antonyms oppose meanings on a relevant scale. A word may have several senses, so neither a prefix nor a dictionary synonym permits every possible substitution.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: help, helpful and unhelpful belong to a word family. A calm room may be described as peaceful; an open door contrasts with a closed door. Replacing peaceful with an unrelated look-alike word would not preserve the description. A change of suffix must still fit the required grammatical position.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Find the base, identify the added part and check the resulting word in its sentence. For synonyms and antonyms, preserve the relevant sense and grammatical construction. Verify unfamiliar forms rather than adding un- or -ness to every word mechanically.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **prefix**: an element added before a base
- **suffix**: an element added after a base
- **synonym in context**: a word with a related meaning in the stated use
- **antonym in context**: a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect


Common mistake: “A prefix always follows its base.” → “prefix: an element added before a base.”. Its position is before the base.

**Card e4-31-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _prefix: an element added before a base._  
> _suffix: an element added after a base._  
> _synonym in context: a word with a related meaning in the stated use._  
> _antonym in context: a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect._  

Check question: `e4-31-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means an element added before a base?

- ◻️ suffix  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an element added after a base, rather than an element added before a base._
- ✅ prefix
- ◻️ antonym in context  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect, rather than an element added before a base._
- ◻️ synonym in context  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a word with a related meaning in the stated use, rather than an element added before a base._

Full working after a second miss:

> Its position is before the base.  
> So the answer is “prefix”.  

**Card e4-31-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _Un- precedes happy in unhappy._  
> _-ful follows help in helpful._  
> _Calm and peaceful can describe the same quiet room._  
> _Open and closed contrast the door’s state._  

Check question: `e4-31-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ synonym in context

- ◻️ prefix  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means an element added before a base. The selected feature instead illustrates a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect._
- ◻️ suffix  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means an element added after a base. The selected feature instead illustrates a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect._
- ✅ antonym in context

Full working after a second miss:

> The opposition is semantic, not merely visual.  
> So the answer is “antonym in context”.  

**Card e4-31-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _A prefix always follows its base. Correction: Its position is before the base._  
> _Every suffix leaves word class unchanged. Correction: Suffixes can alter class as well as meaning._  
> _A synonym must fit every possible sense of a word. Correction: Related meanings remain context-dependent._  
> _Antonyms are defined only by similar spelling. Correction: The opposition is semantic, not merely visual._

## Lesson 32: Writing- A report

Prerequisites: 23, 25, 28, 29, 31 · Skill: e4-32 (Writing a factual report)

**Note** (draft)

> A report presents information for a stated reader and purpose. A heading, introduction, organised findings and a conclusion or recommendations can help. Exact format depends on the task. Separate what was observed from an inference and from what should happen next. Use clear factual wording and appropriate tense. A fictional classroom report must be labelled as such; it should not claim that a real school event or inspection occurred. A report differs from a story seeking suspense or an essay primarily defending a personal preference.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to organise findings and recommendations while separating observation, inference and proposal. Builds on: narrative chronology, audience and formal-purpose writing. Leads to: later evidence-based summaries and reports.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A report presents information for a stated reader and purpose. A heading, introduction, organised findings and a conclusion or recommendations can help. Exact format depends on the task. Separate what was observed from an inference and from what should happen next. Use clear factual wording and appropriate tense. A fictional classroom report must be labelled as such; it should not claim that a real school event or inspection occurred. A report differs from a story seeking suspense or an essay primarily defending a personal preference.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: FICTIONAL CLASSROOM REPORT: Reading-room materials. Purpose: to review the model room’s organisation. Findings: some books were placed away from their labelled shelves, and the borrowing notice was not easily visible. The record does not establish who moved the books. Recommendation: place the notice where readers can see it and agree how returned materials are sorted. A later check should establish whether the arrangement works. Prepared by an imaginary club secretary.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the reader, purpose and permitted information. Group findings logically and link each recommendation to a finding. Do not invent a responsible person when the evidence does not identify one. Check headings, sentence accuracy and whether an inference is being presented as if directly observed.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **finding**: information established by the stated observation or record
- **inference**: an interpretation drawn from evidence rather than directly observed
- **recommendation**: a proposed action responding to the findings
- **report purpose**: the information need that guides the report’s selection and organisation


Common mistake: “A finding must always name a culprit even when none is known.” → “finding: information established by the stated observation or record.”. Report only what the record supports.

**Card e4-32-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _finding: information established by the stated observation or record._  
> _inference: an interpretation drawn from evidence rather than directly observed._  
> _recommendation: a proposed action responding to the findings._  
> _report purpose: the information need that guides the report’s selection and organisation._  

Check question: `e4-32-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means an interpretation drawn from evidence rather than directly observed?

- ◻️ recommendation  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a proposed action responding to the findings, rather than an interpretation drawn from evidence rather than directly observed._
- ◻️ finding  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns information established by the stated observation or record, rather than an interpretation drawn from evidence rather than directly observed._
- ✅ inference
- ◻️ report purpose  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the information need that guides the report’s selection and organisation, rather than an interpretation drawn from evidence rather than directly observed._

Full working after a second miss:

> Distinguish an interpretation from an observation.  
> So the answer is “inference”.  

**Card e4-32-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _The fictional record notes books away from labelled shelves._  
> _A writer suggests a possible organisational cause but marks it as a possibility._  
> _The model proposes making the borrowing notice easier to see._  
> _The model reviews organisation for a fictional club reader._  

Check question: `e4-32-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> The model reviews organisation for a fictional club reader.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ finding  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means information established by the stated observation or record. The selected feature instead illustrates the information need that guides the report’s selection and organisation._
- ✅ report purpose
- ◻️ inference  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means an interpretation drawn from evidence rather than directly observed. The selected feature instead illustrates the information need that guides the report’s selection and organisation._
- ◻️ recommendation  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a proposed action responding to the findings. The selected feature instead illustrates the information need that guides the report’s selection and organisation._

Full working after a second miss:

> Purpose shapes relevant findings and presentation.  
> So the answer is “report purpose”.  

**Card e4-32-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _A finding must always name a culprit even when none is known. Correction: Report only what the record supports._  
> _An inference is automatically a directly witnessed event. Correction: Distinguish an interpretation from an observation._  
> _A recommendation proves the proposed action has already happened. Correction: It proposes a future response, not an accomplished event._  
> _A report must ignore its intended reader and question. Correction: Purpose shapes relevant findings and presentation._  

Check question: `e4-32-repair-card` · spot the error, level 1 · **authored key**

  _↳ explanation: This is correct: a proposed action responding to the findings. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

  _↳ explanation: This is correct: the information need that guides the report’s selection and organisation. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ inference: an interpretation drawn from evidence rather than directly observed.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: an interpretation drawn from evidence rather than directly observed. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “A recommendation proves the proposed action has already happened.”.  
> It proposes a future response, not an accomplished event.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> The fictional record notes books away from labelled shelves. Explain the selected feature.

1. Information established by the stated observation or record.
2. Report only what the record supports.

**Answer:** finding

Findings: some books were placed away from their labelled shelves, and the borrowing notice was not easily visible. The record does not establish who moved the books. Recommendation: place the notice where readers can see it and agree how returned materials are sorted. A later check should establish whether the arrangement works. Prepared by an imaginary club secretary.

- [ ] I distinguish findings, inferences and recommendations.
- [ ] Each recommendation relates to evidence; I invent no culprit.

## Lesson 33: Reading- read a text on hygiene and safety

Prerequisites: 30, 32 · Skill: e4-33 (Reading: hygiene and safety)

**Note** (draft)

> Reading involves following what a passage states, connecting its ideas and distinguishing supported inference from invention. Identify the main idea and locate evidence for each answer. A question may concern a detail, reason, sequence or word in context. If the text omits information, do not supply an invented fact. A plausible outcome is not necessarily a reported result.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to use a passage to distinguish an authorised plan, a precaution and a completed result. Builds on: environmental discussion and factual report writing. Leads to: careful use of instructions and later health/safety vocabulary.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Reading involves following what a passage states, connecting its ideas and distinguishing supported inference from invention. Identify the main idea and locate evidence for each answer. A question may concern a detail, reason, sequence or word in context. If the text omits information, do not supply an invented fact. A plausible outcome is not necessarily a reported result.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original passage: A fictional school club plans to improve access to handwashing. Members check that water and soap are available and ask the teacher which tasks pupils may do. They propose a clear sign near the facility. They do not handle unknown chemicals or sharp waste. The passage reports preparation; it does not claim that a new facility is complete or that handwashing prevents every possible illness.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Point to a phrase supporting the answer and explain any inference. When summarising, preserve who did what and whether an action was planned or completed. These original classroom texts are not extracts from prescribed literature.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **explicit detail**: information directly stated in the passage
- **supported inference**: an interpretation linked to clues in the passage
- **main idea**: the central message organising the passage
- **information not supplied**: a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine


Common mistake: “An explicit detail is always something invented by the reader.” → “explicit detail: information directly stated in the passage.”. Direct information appears in the passage.

**Card e4-33-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _explicit detail: information directly stated in the passage._  
> _supported inference: an interpretation linked to clues in the passage._  
> _main idea: the central message organising the passage._  
> _information not supplied: a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine._  

Check question: `e4-33-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine?

- ◻️ supported inference  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an interpretation linked to clues in the passage, rather than a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine._
- ◻️ explicit detail  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns information directly stated in the passage, rather than a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine._
- ✅ information not supplied
- ◻️ main idea  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the central message organising the passage, rather than a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine._

Full working after a second miss:

> A faithful answer acknowledges missing information.  
> So the answer is “information not supplied”.  

**Card e4-33-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _The reader repeats a place or action the passage actually states._  
> _The reader explains a possible intention using described actions._  
> _A summary brings together the important actions without unrelated additions._  
> _The reader refuses to invent a price or result that is absent._  

Check question: `e4-33-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> The reader explains a possible intention using described actions.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ information not supplied  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a detail the passage does not allow the reader to determine. The selected feature instead illustrates an interpretation linked to clues in the passage._
- ◻️ main idea  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means the central message organising the passage. The selected feature instead illustrates an interpretation linked to clues in the passage._
- ✅ supported inference
- ◻️ explicit detail  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means information directly stated in the passage. The selected feature instead illustrates an interpretation linked to clues in the passage._

Full working after a second miss:

> An inference needs textual support.  
> So the answer is “supported inference”.  

**Card e4-33-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _An explicit detail is always something invented by the reader. Correction: Direct information appears in the passage._  
> _Any private guess is a proven inference. Correction: An inference needs textual support._  
> _A random minor word always gives the complete main idea. Correction: The main idea must represent the relevant whole._  
> _An absent detail must always be replaced with a guessed number. Correction: A faithful answer acknowledges missing information._  

Check question: `e4-33-repair-card` · spot the error, level 1 · **authored key**

> Find the incorrect claim or language form.

- ◻️ main idea: the central message organising the passage.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: the central message organising the passage. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ supported inference: an interpretation linked to clues in the passage.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: an interpretation linked to clues in the passage. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ✅ An explicit detail is always something invented by the reader.  
  _↳ explanation: Direct information appears in the passage._
- ◻️ explicit detail: information directly stated in the passage.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: information directly stated in the passage. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “An explicit detail is always something invented by the reader.”.  
> Direct information appears in the passage.

1. Information directly stated in the passage.

**Practice** `e4-33-apply` · multiple choice, level 2 · **authored key**

> Original passage: A fictional school club plans to improve access to handwashing. Members check that water and soap are available and ask the teacher which tasks pupils may do. They propose a clear sign near the facility. They do not handle unknown chemicals or sharp waste. The passage reports preparation; it does not claim that a new facility is complete or that handwashing prevents every possible illness.
> Which summary is faithful?

- ◻️ The new facility is already complete.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The work is a plan._
- ◻️ All illnesses have ended.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The passage rejects a universal illness guarantee._
- ✅ The club prepares a supervised improvement and recognises safety limits.
- ◻️ The club secretly tests chemicals.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The passage excludes unknown chemical handling._

Full working after a second miss:

> Keep the distinction between preparation and result.  
> So the answer is “The club prepares a supervised improvement and recognises safety limits.”.  

> Original passage: A fictional school club plans to improve access to handwashing. Members check that water and soap are available and ask the teacher which tasks pupils may do. They propose a clear sign near the facility. They do not handle unknown chemicals or sharp waste. The passage reports preparation; it does not claim that a new facility is complete or that handwashing prevents every possible illness.
> Which action is a precaution?

- ◻️ Handling every sharp item to practise.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That contradicts the precaution._
- ✅ Avoiding unknown chemicals and sharp waste.
- ◻️ Mixing unidentified products.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That contradicts the chemical limit._
- ◻️ Ignoring the teacher’s task limits.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The teacher’s authorisation is checked._

Full working after a second miss:

> The stated limit avoids unauthorised hazardous handling.  
> So the answer is “Avoiding unknown chemicals and sharp waste.”.  

**Practice** `e4-33-evidence` · multiple choice, level 2 · **authored key**

> Check this reading claim about the original passage: A missing price or name can be guessed and reported as stated fact.

- ◻️ Yes: a price from another story must apply.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A different story supplies no fact about this passage._
- ◻️ Yes: a familiar name must be the writer’s chosen name.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Familiarity does not provide textual evidence._
- ◻️ Yes: every guess is an explicit detail.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A guess is not direct information._
- ✅ No: report only what the passage supplies or supports.

Full working after a second miss:

> Missing information stays unestablished.  
> So the answer is “No: report only what the passage supplies or supports.”.  

> Check this reading claim about the original passage: A summary should preserve the central actions rather than add an unrelated event.

- ✅ Yes: keep the summary faithful to the passage.
- ◻️ No: summaries must invent a new ending.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A summary does not invent a resolution._
- ◻️ No: any unrelated opinion replaces the main idea.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: An unrelated opinion is not a faithful summary._
- ◻️ No: a summary must be longer than every source text.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Length alone is not the criterion._

Full working after a second miss:

> Select and connect the important points without invention.  
> So the answer is “Yes: keep the summary faithful to the passage.”.
