## Lesson 34: Grammar- compound nouns and collocation

Prerequisites: 16, 31 · Skill: e4-34 (Compound nouns and collocation)

**Note** (draft)

> A compound noun combines elements into a naming expression. It may be written as one word, with a hyphen or as separate words. The spelling is conventional, so check a learner’s dictionary rather than imposing one style on every compound. Many pluralise the last noun, but some mark a different head: mothers-in-law is a familiar example. Collocation concerns conventional combinations more broadly. A phrase can be natural without every combination being a compound noun.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to recognise a compound noun, choose a conventional form and preserve the relevant head in a plural. Builds on: word structure, nouns and natural combinations. Leads to: later formal writing and dictionary-based vocabulary extension.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A compound noun combines elements into a naming expression. It may be written as one word, with a hyphen or as separate words. The spelling is conventional, so check a learner’s dictionary rather than imposing one style on every compound. Many pluralise the last noun, but some mark a different head: mothers-in-law is a familiar example. Collocation concerns conventional combinations more broadly. A phrase can be natural without every combination being a compound noun.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original examples: notebook, bus stop and mother-in-law use different written styles. Their plurals need attention to the naming unit, not a rule that adds -s to the final letter of every expression. A learner takes notes and makes a decision; exchanging every verb with a synonym may spoil the collocation.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify what the compound names and consult its established spelling. Find the head when forming a plural. Keep spelling, word class and meaning distinct; a space does not stop an expression functioning as a compound noun. The lesson checks written recognition, not spoken stress.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **closed compound**: a naming compound written as one word in the model
- **open compound**: a naming compound written as separate words in the model
- **head-based plural**: a plural marking the appropriate central noun in the compound
- **collocational choice**: the conventional combination needed for a natural phrase


Common mistake: “Every compound noun must contain a space.” → “closed compound: a naming compound written as one word in the model.”. Compounds have several conventional written styles.

**Card e4-34-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _closed compound: a naming compound written as one word in the model._  
> _open compound: a naming compound written as separate words in the model._  
> _head-based plural: a plural marking the appropriate central noun in the compound._  
> _collocational choice: the conventional combination needed for a natural phrase._  

Check question: `e4-34-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a naming compound written as separate words in the model?

- ◻️ collocational choice  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the conventional combination needed for a natural phrase, rather than a naming compound written as separate words in the model._
- ◻️ head-based plural  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a plural marking the appropriate central noun in the compound, rather than a naming compound written as separate words in the model._
- ✅ open compound
- ◻️ closed compound  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a naming compound written as one word in the model, rather than a naming compound written as separate words in the model._

Full working after a second miss:

> The naming expression can contain separate written words.  
> So the answer is “open compound”.  

**Card e4-34-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _Notebook is written together in this model._  
> _Bus stop functions as a compound despite the space._  
> _Mother-in-law becomes mothers-in-law._  
> _The learner makes a decision and takes notes._  

Check question: `e4-34-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> The learner makes a decision and takes notes.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ closed compound  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a naming compound written as one word in the model. The selected feature instead illustrates the conventional combination needed for a natural phrase._
- ◻️ head-based plural  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a plural marking the appropriate central noun in the compound. The selected feature instead illustrates the conventional combination needed for a natural phrase._
- ◻️ open compound  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a naming compound written as separate words in the model. The selected feature instead illustrates the conventional combination needed for a natural phrase._
- ✅ collocational choice

Full working after a second miss:

> The whole combination must be checked.  
> So the answer is “collocational choice”.  

**Card e4-34-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _Every compound noun must contain a space. Correction: Compounds have several conventional written styles._  
> _A space proves an expression cannot be a compound noun. Correction: The naming expression can contain separate written words._  
> _Mother-in-law must always become mother-in-laws. Correction: The head mother takes the plural in this model._  
> _Any familiar verb can replace make in make a decision naturally. Correction: The whole combination must be checked._

  _↳ explanation: This is correct: the conventional combination needed for a natural phrase. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

  _↳ explanation: This is correct: a naming compound written as separate words in the model. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “Mother-in-law must always become mother-in-laws.”.  
> The head mother takes the plural in this model.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Notebook is written together in this model. Explain the selected feature.

1. A naming compound written as one word in the model.
2. Compounds have several conventional written styles.

**Answer:** closed compound

**Practice** `e4-34-apply` · multiple choice, level 2 · **authored key**

> Choose the conventional plural in the model.

- ◻️ mother-in-lawes  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That ending does not form the standard plural._
- ◻️ mothers-ins-law  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Do not pluralise the preposition._
- ◻️ mother-in-laws  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The plural belongs on mother._
- ✅ mothers-in-law

Full working after a second miss:

> Mother is the head in this compound.  
> So the answer is “mothers-in-law”.  

> Which expression can be an open compound noun?

- ◻️ has written  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: This is a verb phrase._
- ◻️ very quietly  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: This is an adverbial expression._
- ◻️ although it rains  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: This is a dependent clause._
- ✅ bus stop

Full working after a second miss:

> Bus stop names one kind of place despite its space.  
> So the answer is “bus stop”.  

**Practice** `e4-34-link` · matching, level 2 · **authored key**

- open compound → **Bus stop functions as a compound despite the space.**
- collocational choice → **The learner makes a decision and takes notes.**
- closed compound → **Notebook is written together in this model.**
